# Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement

Mehryar Mohri    Jon Schneider    Yifan Wu

###### Abstract

Self-improvement is a critical capability for large language models and
other intelligent systems, enabling them to refine their behavior and
internal consistency without external supervision. Despite its
importance, prior approaches largely rely on empirical heuristics and
lack formal guarantees. In this paper, we propose a principled framework
for self-improvement based on the concept of _coherence_, which requires
that a model’s outputs remain consistent under task-preserving
transformations of the input.

We formalize this concept using projection-based mechanisms that update
a baseline model to be coherent while remaining as close as possible to
its original behavior. We provide rigorous theoretical guarantees that
these mechanisms achieve _monotonic improvement_, measured by a
reduction in expected Bregman divergence. Our analysis is comprehensive,
covering both _direct_ and _two-step_ projection methods, and robustly
extends these guarantees to non-realizable settings, empirical
(finite-sample) distributions, and relaxed coherence constraints.

Furthermore, we establish a general _characterization theorem_, showing
that any mechanism with similar provable improvement guarantees must
inherently conform to a coherence-based structure. This culminates in
rigidity results under the demand for universal improvement,
establishing coherence as a fundamental and, in a formal sense,
necessary principle for provable self-improvement.

## 1 Introduction

The ability to self-improve is a central and defining challenge in the
design of large language models (LLMs) and other advanced intelligent
systems. Beyond being a desirable feature, self-improvement is essential
for developing models that are internally consistent, robust, and
capable of adapting to complex or evolving tasks. In domains ranging
from robotics to scientific discovery and natural language processing,
the capacity to autonomously enhance performance without constant,
granular human supervision can dramatically improve reliability, safety,
and overall task effectiveness.

Despite its critical importance, the field has largely been guided by
empirical heuristics. This creates a significant gap: without a formal
understanding of _why_ a given self-improvement mechanism works, there
is no rigorous guarantee that it will not fail, degrade performance in
subtle ways, or introduce new, unforeseen inconsistencies. This paper
aims to bridge this gap by proposing a principled, mathematical
framework for self-improvement grounded in the fundamental concept of
_coherence_.

Our core thesis is that a model’s internal consistency is a powerful and
measurable proxy for its quality. We define coherence as the requirement
that a model produce consistent outputs for inputs that are equivalent
under a task-preserving transformation. For example, a model should
ideally output the same answer distribution for a question $`x`$ and its
semantic paraphrase $`\Phi(x)`$. An _incoherent_ model, one that
contradicts itself by outputting different answers for equivalent
inputs, is demonstrably flawed. We posit that the process of actively
reducing this internal incoherence provides a provable, non-heuristic
path to model improvement.

We formalize this intuition by defining the set of all coherent models,
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$. Our self-improvement mechanisms are then
formulated as _projections_: given a baseline model $`\pi_{0}`$, we
project it onto this coherent set to find an improved model
$`\widehat{\pi}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$ that is as close as
possible to $`\pi_{0}`$. To measure this "closeness," we use the
geometric language of _Bregman divergences_, a broad class of measures
that includes the KL divergence and squared Euclidean distance. This
geometric approach allows us to provide formal, non-asymptotic
guarantees of monotonic improvement.

This principled, projection-based framework stands in contrast to many
existing approaches that, while often empirically successful, are guided
by different principles. Recent work has explored diverse strategies,
from generating synthetic instruction data ([Wang et al.,
2023b](#bib.bib4); [Wang et al., 2022](#bib.bib5); [Chen et al.,
2024](#bib.bib6)) to inference-time verification like the _sharpening
mechanism_ ([Huang et al., 2024](#bib.bib18)). Other approaches focus on
data augmentation through paraphrasing ([Xu et al., 2023](#bib.bib7)),
iterative self-feedback loops ([Madaan et al., 2023](#bib.bib8)), and
adversarial debates ([Chen et al., 2023](#bib.bib2)). While these
studies illustrate a spectrum of powerful strategies, our work
complements this line of research by providing a rigorous theoretical
foundation for a class of mechanisms with provable guarantees.

This paper provides a comprehensive theoretical analysis of
coherence-based self-improvement. The argument is structured to build
from foundational concepts to our main theoretical results.

Foundations
([Sections 2](#S2 "2 Preliminaries ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")
and [3](#S3 "3 Coherence as a Principle of Self-Improvement ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
We first establish our mathematical language.
[Section 2](#S2 "2 Preliminaries ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")
introduces the notation and probabilistic setting, and then provides a
detailed review of Bregman divergences and Legendre functions, which
form the core geometric toolkit for our entire analysis.
[Section 3](#S3 "3 Coherence as a Principle of Self-Improvement ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")
then formalizes our central concept. We introduce _invariance mappings_
($`\Phi`$) and use them to define the sets of coherent models
($`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$,
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger}`$,
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger\dagger}`$), establishing their key
properties (e.g., as convex cones or linear subspaces). This section
culminates in the formal problem formulation: self-improvement as a
constrained optimization problem.

Direct Coherence Projection
([Section 4](#S4 "4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
This section introduces and analyzes our first mechanism, the _direct
projection_ of $`\pi_{0}`$ onto the target set
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi`$.

- •
  We begin by establishing our main _improvement guarantee_
  ([Section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Improvement guarantees ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")),
  proving via the Pythagorean theorem that this projection monotonically
  decreases the divergence to an ideal coherent model $`\pi^{*}`$. This
  uses a key lemma that proves several important properties of the
  expected Bregman divergence between conditional probabilities under
  the input distribution
  ([Section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Bregman divergence expectation ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
- •
  We then extend this central result to more realistic scenarios,
  providing guarantees for the _non-realizable setting_ (where
  $`\pi^{*}`$ is not perfectly coherent,
  [Section 4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Improvement guarantees in non-realizable setting ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")),
  the _empirical setting_ (where projections are computed on a finite
  sample,
  [Section 4.4](#S4.SS4 "4.4 Improvement guarantees for empirical Bregman–projection ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")),
  and for _relaxed constraints_ (where strict coherence is softened to
  an inequality,
  [Section 4.5](#S4.SS5 "4.5 Improvement guarantees for relaxed constraints ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
- •
  The section concludes with a study of guarantees for a convex family
  of Bregman divergence generators. We first analyze a natural robust
  solution based on a minimax optimization and show that in general it
  cannot yield a general improvement
  [Section 4.6](#S4.SS6 "4.6 Failure of Pythagorean improvement for minimax projections ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement").
  Next, we analyze universal improvement and present impossibility
  results suggesting that it can only be achieved under very restrictive
  assumptions
  [Section 4.7](#S4.SS7 "4.7 Uniform improvement guarantees and impossibility results ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement").

Two-Step Coherence Projection
([Section 5](#S5 "5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
This section develops an alternative _two-step_ mechanism, which first
projects $`\pi_{0}`$ onto the unconstrained coherence set
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger}`$ and then onto the final target
set.

- •
  We first prove a remarkable _equivalence result_
  ([Sections 5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Pythagorean theorem equality ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")
  and [5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Comparison of direct and two-step projections ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")),
  showing that for a broad and common class of Bregman divergences
  (including Euclidean, KL, and all separable or quadratic generators),
  this two-step projection is _identical_ to the direct projection.
- •
  We then show the utility of this alternative view by using it to
  derive a new set of theoretical guarantees. This includes an
  alternative improvement bound
  ([Section 5.3](#S5.SS3 "5.3 Improvement guarantees via two step-projection ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")),
  explicit bounds in terms of Hellinger distance for relative entropy
  ([Section 5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Improvement guarantees for relative entropy two-step projection ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")),
  and a striking _maximin property_ showing our solution is optimal
  against the worst-case ideal model $`\pi^{*}`$
  ([Section 5.5](#S5.SS5 "5.5 Maximin properties ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).

Characterization and Rigidity
([Section 6](#S6 "6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
Finally, this section provides the theoretical capstone of the paper,
proving that our coherence-based framework is not merely one possible
method, but is foundational to provable self-improvement.

- •
  We first show that _any_ mechanism that satisfies a similar
  improvement guarantee must, in effect, be a Bregman projection onto a
  coherence-like set defined by its own level-sets
  ([Section 6.1](#S6.SS1 "6.1 Characterization for a single divergence ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
- •
  We then prove a series of powerful _rigidity theorems_
  ([Section 6.2](#S6.SS2 "6.2 Rigidity under all divergences – Topological proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
  These show that under the strong requirement of universal improvement
  across _all_ Legendre divergences in a family of divergences generated
  by a convex family, the mechanism’s output structure is forced to be
  rigid: it must be block-constant on a _single, universal partition_,
  independent of the specific divergence. This reveals that any such
  mechanism is inherently aligned with the coherence-projection
  framework we introduce.

Taken together, these results build a comprehensive theoretical case for
coherence as a principled and provably effective mechanism for
self-improvement.

### 1.1 Related Work

Our work sits at the intersection of several active research areas:
heuristic self-improvement, invariance in machine learning, and the
geometric theory of projections. We situate our contribution relative to
these fields.

Heuristic Self-Improvement and Self-Correction. A dominant paradigm for
improving LLMs without human labels is to fine-tune them on their own
outputs, a process often called self-instruction ([Wang et al.,
2023b](#bib.bib4); [Wang et al., 2022](#bib.bib5)). This typically
involves a "judge" model (which can be the model itself) to filter,
score, or refine generations ([Madaan et al., 2023](#bib.bib8); [Huang
et al., 2023](#bib.bib9); [Zheng et al., 2023](#bib.bib30)). This
approach has been formalized as Reinforcement Learning from AI Feedback
(RLAIF), where the model learns from its own self-generated rewards
([Yuan et al., 2024](#bib.bib25)), or through principled self-critique,
as in Constitutional AI ([Bai et al., 2022](#bib.bib75)).

While empirically powerful, these methods rely on the model’s own, often
biased ([Xu et al., 2024](#bib.bib26)), assessment of "quality" or
"harmlessness." This can lead to instability, "model collapse" ([Hataya
et al., 2023](#bib.bib27); [Martínez et al., 2023](#bib.bib28);
[Bertrand et al., 2024](#bib.bib29); [Shumailov et al.,
2024](#bib.bib1)), or reinforcement of the model’s own blind spots. Our
framework differs fundamentally. Instead of relying on a subjective
"quality" score, we use the objective, formal criterion of _coherence_
as the improvement signal. We do not ask the model to _judge_ its
outputs, but rather enforce that its outputs be _mathematically
consistent_ under transformation, providing a more robust, non-heuristic
foundation for improvement.

Inference-Time Mechanisms and Self-Consistency. A separate line of
research modifies the decoding process at inference time. This includes
generating candidate sets via sampling ([Ji et al., 2023](#bib.bib22);
[Finlayson et al., 2024](#bib.bib21)) or search ([Zhang et al.,
2023](#bib.bib23)), and then re-weighting or selecting outputs based on
model-internal signals ([Fu et al., 2025](#bib.bib24); [Chuang et al.,
2024](#bib.bib20)) or external constraints ([Lu et al.,
2022](#bib.bib19)).

Perhaps the most relevant work in this area is _self-consistency_ ([Wang
et al., 2023a](#bib.bib74)), which improves reasoning by sampling
multiple outputs from varied prompts (e.g., chain-of-thought variants
([Wei et al., 2022](#bib.bib76))) and taking a majority vote.
Self-consistency _leverages_ a model’s incoherence, that is, its
tendency to give different answers to equivalent prompts, to find a more
robust aggregate answer. Our work is the conceptual inverse: we seek to
_eliminate_ this incoherence from the model’s parameters directly, not
merely average it out at inference time. Our coherence projection can be
seen as a training-time mechanism to "bake in" self-consistency,
yielding a model that is inherently more reliable. The theoretical work
of [Huang et al. (2024)](#bib.bib18) on tuning a model to learn its
best-of-N (BoN) generations is also related, though our focus is on
enforcing coherence across model outputs, rather than amplifying a
model-defined "best" output.

Invariance, Data Augmentation, and Self-Distillation. The use of
label-preserving transformations, or _data augmentation_, is a
cornerstone of modern machine learning, especially in vision
([Krizhevsky et al., 2012](#bib.bib31); [Cubuk et al.,
2019](#bib.bib32); [Cubuk et al., 2020](#bib.bib33); [Chen et al.,
2020](#bib.bib34); [DeVries and Taylor, 2017](#bib.bib35)). In NLP, this
includes paraphrasing, back-translation, and token-level perturbations
([Wei and Zou, 2019](#bib.bib36); [Sennrich et al., 2016](#bib.bib37);
[Wang et al., 2018](#bib.bib39); [Xie et al., 2020](#bib.bib38); [Yang
et al., 2025](#bib.bib53)). These methods all heuristically enforce
invariance by showing the model examples of inputs ($`x`$) and
transformations ($`\Phi(x)`$) that share a label. Our work provides a
formal, geometric basis for this intuition. We frame the goal not as
"generating more data", but as a principled _projection_ onto the set of
coherent models $`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$. The work of [Xie et al.
(2020)](#bib.bib38), which adds an unsupervised coherence loss, and
[Maini et al. (2024)](#bib.bib52), which studies pre-training with
paraphrases, are particularly close practical realizations of this
concept, for which we now provide a general theoretical guarantee.

This perspective also connects to _self-distillation_ ([Hinton et al.,
2015](#bib.bib43); [Sanh et al., 2019](#bib.bib54); [Gerstgrasser et
al., 2024](#bib.bib57); [Kim et al., 2025](#bib.bib42); [Gu et al.,
2024](#bib.bib56); [Agarwal et al., 2024](#bib.bib55)). The theory of
[Allen-Zhu and Li (2023)](#bib.bib58) suggests that gains from
self-distillation can arise from an "implicit ensemble" effect. Our
coherence projection offers a precise formulation of this: it finds a
single model $`\widehat{\pi}`$ that is the Bregman centroid of the
"ensemble" of incoherent views (e.g., $`\pi_{0}(x)`$ and
$`\pi_{0}(\Phi(x))`$), thereby distilling their shared knowledge into
one consistent model.

Bregman Projections in Machine Learning. The mathematical tools we use
are grounded in a rich history of Bregman divergences in machine
learning. Bregman divergences are central to maximum entropy models
([Collins et al., 2002](#bib.bib63); [Dudík et al., 2007](#bib.bib68);
[Altun and Smola, 2006](#bib.bib69); [Banerjee et al.,
2007](#bib.bib70); [Frongillo and Reid, 2013](#bib.bib71); [Harremoës,
2016](#bib.bib72); [Nock et al., 2016](#bib.bib73); [Cortes et al.,
2015](#bib.bib67); [Mohri et al., 2018](#bib.bib14)), boosting
algorithms ([Collins et al., 2002](#bib.bib63); [Kivinen and Warmuth,
1999](#bib.bib64); [Warmuth et al., 2008](#bib.bib65); [Tsuda et al.,
2005](#bib.bib66); [Lafferty, 1999](#bib.bib59); [Murata et al.,
2004](#bib.bib60); [Liu and Vemuri, 2011](#bib.bib61); [Rezaei et al.,
2021](#bib.bib62)), clustering ([Banerjee et al., 2005](#bib.bib40)),
and Bayesian estimation ([Frigyik et al., 2008](#bib.bib41)). Our
contribution is to apply this powerful geometric tool to the problem of
model self-improvement, using projections onto coherence-defined sets as
the core mechanism for provably enhancing model consistency and
performance.

## 2 Preliminaries

This section introduces notation, probabilistic setting, and key
properties of Bregman divergences, which will be used throughout.

### 2.1 Notation and probabilistic setting

Let $`\Sigma`$ be a finite alphabet (set of tokens). Prompts are
sequences $`x\in\mathscr{X}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}`$, and outputs lie in
$`\mathscr{Y}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}`$. A language model is a conditional
probability distribution

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|     |

       ``` math
       \pi\colon\mathscr{X}\to\Delta(\mathscr{Y}),
       ```                                          |     |

where $`\Delta(\mathscr{Y})`$ denotes the probability simplex over
$`\mathscr{Y}`$. For any prompt $`x`$, we write $`\pi(x)`$ for the
corresponding distribution over outputs.

To simplify exposition, we assume throughout that $`\mathscr{X}`$ and
$`\mathscr{Y}`$ are finite subsets of $`\Sigma^{*}`$. The arguments
extend to the common setting where $`\mathscr{X}`$ is countable and
$`\mathscr{Y}`$ is finite, and further to countable $`\mathscr{Y}`$
under standard compactness assumptions on the model class.

We write
$`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}=\big(\Delta(\mathscr{Y})\big)^{\mathscr{X}}`$ for
the set of all conditional distribution models. Since $`\mathscr{Y}`$ is
finite, $`\Delta(\mathscr{Y})`$ is a compact convex subset of a
finite-dimensional Euclidean space. When $`\mathscr{X}`$ is finite,
$`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$ is therefore a finite product of compact sets
(where all standard topologies such as pointwise, uniform, etc., are
equivalent), and hence a compact metric space. For countable
$`\mathscr{X}`$, Tychonoff’s theorem guarantees that
$`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$ remains compact under the product topology
(equivalently, the topology of pointwise convergence).

We also define the extended space of unnormalized models
$`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}^{\dagger}=\big(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\mathscr{Y}}\big)^{\mathscr{X}}`$,
where $`\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\mathscr{Y}}\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}`$ with
$`d=|\mathscr{Y}|`$. When $`n=|\mathscr{X}|`$ is finite, this gives
$`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}^{\dagger}\cong(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})^{n}=\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n\times d}`$,
a closed and convex cone in a finite-dimensional vector space. Since it
is unbounded, $`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}^{\dagger}`$ is not compact.

Throughout, we work with a closed and convex family of models
$`\Pi\subseteq\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$. Compactness of
$`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$ implies that any closed subset $`\Pi`$ is also
compact.

Finally, expectations are taken with respect to a fixed prompt
distribution $`\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}`$ over $`\mathscr{X}`$. We will
assume, without loss of generality that
$`\supp(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}})=\mathscr{X}`$.

### 2.2 Bregman divergences and Legendre functions

In this section, we review the definitions and key properties of Bregman
divergences and Legendre-type functions. These tools provide the
foundational geometry for our self-improvement mechanisms, in particular
for computing centroids, projections, and bounding errors via
Fenchel-Bregman inequalities. We focus on the differentiable case
corresponding to Legendre-type generators, while noting the general
subgradient-based definitions for completeness.

#### 2.2.1 Bregman divergences

We adopt the standard definition of Bregman divergence following
[Rockafellar (1997)](#bib.bib10) (see also ([Bregman, 1967](#bib.bib47);
[Censor and Lent, 1981](#bib.bib48))).

###### Definition 1 (Bregman divergences).

Let $`F\colon\mathscr{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}`$ be a
convex function defined on a convex set $`\mathscr{K}`$. The _Bregman
divergence_ $`\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q})`$ is defined for any
point $`\mathsf{p}\in\mathscr{K}`$ and $`\mathsf{q}`$ in the interior of
$`\dom(F)`$ where $`F`$ admits a sub-gradient, as the gap between
$`F(\mathsf{p})`$ and the first-order approximation of $`F`$ at
$`\mathsf{q}`$, evaluated at $`\mathsf{p}`$:

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})=F(\mathsf{p})-F(\mathsf{q})-\left\langle g_{\mathsf{q}},\mathsf{p}-\mathsf{q}\right\rangle,
``` |  |

where $`g_{\mathsf{q}}`$ is any element of $`\partial F(\mathsf{q})`$,
the sub-differential of $`F`$ at $`\mathsf{q}`$.

This definition can be further extended, for example by considering
directional derivatives instead of sub-gradients. Throughout most of
this work, we restrict attention to the common case where $`F`$ is
differentiable on the interior of its domain. Then,
$`\partial F(q)=\left\{\nabla F(q)\right\}`$ and the divergence takes
the familiar simpler form:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})=F(\mathsf{p})-F(\mathsf{q})-\left\langle\nabla F(\mathsf{q}),\mathsf{p}-\mathsf{q}\right\rangle.
``` |  |

Our primary setting is that of probability distributions over a finite
label set $`\mathscr{Y}`$, represented by elements of the probability
simplex $`\Delta(\mathscr{Y})`$ (or $`\Delta_{d}`$ where
$`d=|\mathscr{Y}|`$). These distributions arise as outputs of
conditional models $`\pi\colon\mathscr{X}\to\Delta(\mathscr{Y})`$. To
ensure Bregman divergences are well-defined for such outputs, we only
need to make a minimal set of assumptions: We assume the domain of
$`F`$, $`\mathscr{K}`$, contains the probability simplex
$`\Delta(\mathscr{Y})`$, and that its topological interior,
$`\Int(\mathscr{K})`$, contains the relative interior of the simplex,
$`\relint(\Delta(\mathscr{Y}))`$. A simple domain that satisfies this is
the unit hypercube $`\mathscr{K}=[0,1]^{d}`$, since the simplex
$`\Delta(\mathscr{Y})`$ is fully contained within $`\mathscr{K}`$. The
relative interior of the simplex (where $`\mathsf{q}_{i}>0`$ and
$`\sum\mathsf{q}_{i}=1`$) is contained within the topological interior
of the hypercube, $`\text{int}(\mathscr{K})=(0,1)^{d}`$. Under these
minimal conditions, $`\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})`$ is
well-defined for any $`\mathsf{p}\in\Delta(\mathscr{Y})`$ and any
$`\mathsf{q}`$ in the relative interior of the simplex.

Under these standard assumptions, Bregman divergences admit several
well-known properties (see [Rockafellar (1997)](#bib.bib10) or
Appendix E of ([Mohri et al., 2018](#bib.bib14)), and ([Nielsen,
2020](#bib.bib51)) for an introductory text):

- •
  Non-Negativity: $`B_{F}\geq 0`$.
- •
  Convexity in the first argument: For a fixed $`\mathsf{q}`$, the
  function $`\mathsf{p}\mapsto B_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})`$ is
  convex in $`\mathsf{p}`$.
- •
  Asymmetry: In general,
  $`B_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})\neq B_{F}(\mathsf{q}\parallel\mathsf{p})`$.
- •
  Three-Point Identity (or triangular-type identity): For any
  $`\mathsf{p}\in\mathscr{K}`$ and any
  $`\mathsf{q},\mathsf{r}\in\text{int}(\mathscr{K})=\Omega`$, the
  following identity holds:

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  \mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{r})+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{r}\parallel\mathsf{q})=\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})+\langle\nabla F(\mathsf{q})-\nabla F(\mathsf{r}),\mathsf{p}-\mathsf{r}\rangle.
  ``` |  |
- •
  Projection: Let $`\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{K}`$ be a non-empty,
  closed, and convex set, then the projection
  $`\Proj_{\mathscr{C}}^{F}(\mathsf{q})=\argmin_{\mathsf{p}\in\mathscr{C}}\mathsf{B}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})`$
  is well defined for any $`\mathsf{q}`$ allowed in the definition of
  $`\mathsf{B}_{F}`$. In our setting, where $`\mathscr{C}`$ is a subset
  of $`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$, $`\mathscr{C}`$ is a compact set as a
  closed subset of $`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$ and continuity in
  $`\mathsf{p}`$ ensures existence of a minimizer.
- •
  Generalized Pythagorean Theorem: Let $`\mathscr{C}`$ be a non-empty,
  closed, and convex set, and let
  $`\overline{\mathsf{q}}=\Proj_{\mathscr{C}}^{F}(\mathsf{q})`$. Then,
  for any point $`\mathsf{p}\in\mathscr{C}`$, the following inequality
  holds:

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  \mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})\geq\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\overline{\mathsf{q}})+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\overline{\mathsf{q}}\parallel\mathsf{q}).
  ``` |  |

  The theorem holds with equality if the set $`\mathscr{C}`$ is an
  affine space.
- •
  Positive linearity in the generator: if $`F`$ and $`G`$ are two convex
  functions and $`\alpha,\beta>0`$, then:
  $`\mathsf{B}_{\alpha F+\beta G}=\alpha\mathsf{B}_{F}+\beta\mathsf{B}_{G}`$.

#### 2.2.2 Legendre functions

We will often consider the special case where our generating function
$`F\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathbb{R}`$ is of *Legendre type* (or simply
*Legendre*). The following definition is based on ([Rockafellar,
1997](#bib.bib10)).

###### Definition 2 (Functions of Legendre Type).

A proper, convex, and closed function
$`F\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathbb{R}`$ is of *Legendre type* if its
behavior on its open interior $`\Omega=\text{int}(\mathscr{K})`$ is as
follows:

1.  1.
    $`F`$ is differentiable on $`\Omega`$.
2.  2.
    The gradient mapping $`\nabla F\colon\Omega\to\Int(\dom(F^{*}))`$ is
    a bijection.

The bijection property is equivalent to $`F`$ being strictly convex on
$`\Omega`$ and *steep* (that is for any sequence
$`\mathsf{p}_{k}\in\Omega`$ converging to a boundary point
$`\mathsf{p}_{\partial}\in\partial\mathscr{K}`$, the norm of the
gradient explodes: $`\|\nabla F(\mathsf{p}_{k})\|\to+\infty`$), which
ensures $`\nabla F`$ is surjective.

When $`F`$ is of Legendre type, its Fenchel conjugate $`F^{*}`$ is also
of Legendre type, and a number of standard properties hold:

- •
  Uniqueness of Projection: For any non-empty, closed, and convex set
  $`\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{K}`$, the Bregman projection of a point
  $`\mathsf{q}\in\Omega`$ onto $`\mathscr{C}`$,
  $`\Proj_{\mathscr{C}}^{F}(\mathsf{q})`$, exists and is unique.
- •
  Fenchel-Young Equality: For any $`\mathsf{p}\in\Omega`$,

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  F(\mathsf{p})+F^{*}(\nabla F(\mathsf{p}))=\langle\mathsf{p},\nabla F(\mathsf{p})\rangle.
  ``` |  |
- •
  Divergence Duality: For any $`\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q}\in\Omega`$,

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  \mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})=\mathsf{B}_{F^{*}}\left(\nabla F(\mathsf{q})\parallel\nabla F(\mathsf{p})\right).
  ``` |  |

The negative entropy function and the norm squared functions are
examples of Legendre functions generating the (unnormalized)
$`\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}`$ and the Euclidean distance squared Bregman
divergences.

#### 2.2.3 Centroid properties

Bregman divergences admit closed-form expressions for centroids and
projections, which are essential for our self-improvement mechanisms, in
particular for our two-step projection method. These results allow us to
characterize the minimizers of weighted sums of divergences.

###### Lemma 3.

Let $`\lambda\in\Delta_{p}`$ and
$`\mathsf{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{p}_{p}\in\mathscr{K}`$, and
$`\mathsf{q}\in\Int(\dom(F))`$. Then, the following holds:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}_{k}\parallel\mathsf{q})-\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\mathsf{p}_{k}\parallel\mathsf{q}\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}F(\mathsf{p}_{k})-F\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\mathsf{p}_{k}\right).
``` |  |

This identity shows that the Bregman divergence from a weighted
combination of points to a reference $`\mathsf{q}`$ decomposes in a way
independent of $`\mathsf{q}`$. This property underlies the computation
of centroids and justifies that Bregman projections can be expressed in
terms of gradient averages.

###### Lemma 4.

Let $`F`$ be Legendre and
$`\mathsf{q}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{q}_{p}\in\Omega`$ with weights
$`\lambda\in\Delta_{p}`$. Then, the minimizer of
$`\mathsf{p}\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q}_{k})`$
over $`\Omega`$ satisfies

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\nabla F(\mathsf{p}^{*})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\nabla F(\mathsf{q}_{k}),\quad\text{or equivalently }\mathsf{p}^{*}=(\nabla F)^{-1}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\nabla F(\mathsf{q}_{k})\right).
``` |  |

If $`\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{K}`$ is closed and convex, the unique
minimizer over $`\mathscr{C}`$ is the Bregman projection of
$`\mathsf{p}^{*}`$ onto $`\mathscr{C}`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{p}^{*}_{\mathscr{C}}=\argmin_{\mathsf{p}\in\mathscr{C}}\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{p}^{*}).
``` |  |

This lemma provides an explicit formula for the Bregman centroid and its
projection onto convex sets, which is fundamental for constructing
improved models that satisfy coherence or other convex constraints.

###### Corollary 5.

Let $`\mathsf{p}^{*}`$ and $`\mathsf{p}^{*}_{\mathscr{C}}`$ be as above.
Then,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\min_{\mathsf{p}\in K}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q}_{k})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}F^{*}(\nabla F(\mathsf{p}_{k}))-F^{*}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\nabla F(\mathsf{q}_{k})\right)+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}^{*}_{\mathscr{C}}\parallel\mathsf{p}^{*}).
``` |  |

This corollary provides a decomposition of the minimum Bregman
divergence over a convex set into a centroid term and a projection term,
which is particularly useful for establishing theoretical guarantees of
projection-based self-improvement, especially in the context of our
two-step projection method.

#### 2.2.4 Fenchel-Bregman inequality

This general inequality bounds the inner product of differences by
Bregman divergences and is widely used to control error terms in proofs.

###### Lemma 6.

For any Legendre function $`F`$ and $`u,v\in\Omega=\Int(\dom(F))`$ with
$`\alpha\in\Int(\dom(F^{*}))=\nabla F(\Omega)`$ and
$`\beta=\nabla F(v)`$, the following inequality holds:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\langle u-v,\alpha-\beta\rangle\leq\mathsf{B}_{F}(u\parallel v)+\mathsf{B}_{F^{*}}(\alpha\parallel\beta).
``` |  |

This inequality is a key tool for bounding inner-product terms in
analysis of projections and improvement mechanisms. It generalizes the
familiar Cauchy-Schwarz-type control using the geometry of Bregman
divergences. The proofs for all these results are given in
Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Proofs of Bregman Divergence Results ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement").

## 3 Coherence as a Principle of Self-Improvement

We now introduce the key conceptual framework underlying our
self-improvement methodology. At its core is the principle of
*coherence*, which formalizes the intuition that models should produce
consistent outputs for inputs that are equivalent under task-preserving
transformations.

### 3.1 Invariance mappings

Let $`\Phi\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{X}`$ be a mapping on inputs
(e.g., prompts). We call $`\Phi`$ an *invariance mapping* if, under an
ideal model $`\pi^{*}`$, outputs are preserved:

|     |                                                                 |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \pi^{*}(x)=\pi^{*}(\Phi(x))\quad\text{for all }x\in\mathscr{X},
       ```                                                              |     |

where equality is interpreted under a task-preserving equivalence
relation (syntactic, semantic, answer-based, or distributional).

For example, if $`x`$ is a well-formed English sentence, its passive
form $`x^{\prime}`$ should lead to the same model output. Semantically
equivalent paraphrases are similarly expected to produce consistent
outputs. This idea extends beyond text: two images differing only by a
90-degree rotation typically depict the same object and should yield
similar outputs in a vision model. Such examples illustrate the
generality of invariance mappings as a way to capture task-preserving
symmetries.

Conceptually, these mappings are close to Zellig Harris’s
*transformations* ([Harris, 1968](#bib.bib3)) in his mathematical theory
of language, in that $`\Phi`$ is a formal operation on linguistic
objects that preserves meaning. This connection emphasizes that
invariance mappings are not arbitrary but grounded in a formal structure
of equivalence.

Throughout this paper, we assume that $`\Phi`$ is an *involution*, i.e.,

|     |                                              |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \Phi(\Phi(x))=x\quad\forall x\in\mathscr{X},
       ```                                           |     |

which simplifies notation and analysis. More generally, our results
extend to the case where $`\Phi`$ has a *finite orbit*, i.e., there
exists $`k\in\mathbb{N}`$ such that $`\Phi^{k}(x)=x`$ for each
$`x\in\mathscr{X}`$. An even broader formulation arises by partitioning
$`\mathscr{X}`$ into equivalence classes $`\mathscr{X}_{i}`$,
$`i\in I`$, such that all inputs in the same class are treated as
equivalent. In this view, invariance requires consistency of model
outputs within each class. Beyond textual data, equivalence classes can
represent semantically or functionally equivalent inputs in images,
audio, or structured data, providing a unifying framework across
modalities.

Invariance mappings are not limited to passive-active transformations.
They can also capture other structural or semantic transformations such
as negation, interrogative forms, stylistic variations, or
domain-specific invariances, making them a flexible foundation for
enforcing coherence.

### 3.2 Coherent models

A model $`\pi`$ is said to be *coherent* if it satisfies

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\pi(x)=\pi(\Phi(x))\quad\forall x\in\mathscr{X},\ \forall\Phi\text{ invariance mapping.}
``` |  |

Coherence formalizes a natural desideratum for models: inputs that are
semantically or structurally equivalent should yield consistent outputs.
By enforcing coherence, we can improve model reliability, robustness,
and internal consistency, which are essential for self-improvement
mechanisms.

We fix an invariance mapping $`\Phi`$ throughout. We first define the
family of all coherent functions mapping from $`\mathscr{X}`$ to
$`\mathbb{R}^{d}`$

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger\dagger}=\left\{\pi\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\pi(x)=\pi(\Phi(x))\text{ for all }x\in\mathscr{X}\right\}.
``` |  |

Equivalently, $`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger\dagger}`$ consists
of functions that are constant on the orbits of $`\Phi`$. Each equality
constraint $`\pi(x)=\pi(\Phi(x))`$ is linear, hence
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger\dagger}`$ is a closed linear
subspace of $`(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathscr{X}}`$, and therefore convex.
The set of coherent (unnormalized) models is given by

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger}=\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger\dagger}\cap\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}^{\dagger}.
``` |  |

Since $`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}^{\dagger}`$ is a closed convex cone and
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger\dagger}`$ is a closed linear
subspace, $`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger}`$ is a closed convex
cone. It is not an affine set unless $`\Phi`$ is the identity mapping,
in which case all models are coherent. Finally, we define the family of
coherent conditional probability models as

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}=\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger}\cap\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}.
``` |  |

Since $`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$ is compact and
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$ is a closed subset of
$`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$, $`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$ is compact.
Convexity follows since both $`\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$ and
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger}`$ are convex.

### 3.3 Problem formulation

Let $`\pi_{0}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$ be a base model and
$`\Phi\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{X}`$ an invariance mapping. Our goal
is to construct a model $`\pi\in\Pi`$ that simultaneously satisfies two
desiderata:

1.  1.
    Closeness to the base model: $`\pi`$ should remain close to
    $`\pi_{0}`$ under a chosen Bregman divergence $`\mathsf{B}_{F}`$,
    ensuring that improvements preserve the original model’s knowledge.
2.  2.
    Coherence: $`\pi`$ should be (approximately) invariant under
    $`\Phi`$, that is,

    |     |                                                               |     |
    |-----|---------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
    |     |
           ``` math
           \pi(x)\approx\pi(\Phi(x))\quad\text{for all }x\in\mathscr{X}.
           ```                                                            |     |

Formally, this can be expressed as a constrained optimization problem:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\min_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi\parallel\pi_{0})\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\pi(x)=\pi(\Phi(x))\text{ (or a relaxed version) for all }x.
``` |  |

This formulation provides the foundation for our analysis of
projection-based improvement mechanisms. By minimizing a Bregman
divergence while enforcing (or relaxing) coherence constraints, we
obtain models that are guaranteed to improve in a principled manner
while respecting the structure of the original model and task-specific
invariances.

## 4 Direct Coherence Projection

In this section, we study Bregman projections as a mechanism for
enforcing coherence in conditional models. We begin with a key lemma
that characterizes the expected Bregman divergence between conditional
probabilities under the input distribution
(Subsection [4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Bregman divergence expectation ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
Building on this, we establish general improvement guarantees for
Bregman projections onto coherence sets and, more generally, onto
arbitrary convex sets, under the assumption that the reference
distribution $`\pi^{*}`$ is coherent
(Subsection [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Improvement guarantees ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
We then relax this assumption and analyze the setting where $`\pi^{*}`$
may not be coherent but lies close to the coherent set
(Subsection [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Improvement guarantees in non-realizable setting ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).

In practical applications, projection algorithms operate on empirical
distributions. Accordingly, we provide guarantees for empirical Bregman
projections, where the divergence is estimated from finite data
(Subsection [4.4](#S4.SS4 "4.4 Improvement guarantees for empirical Bregman–projection ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
We give an extensive discussion of these results and present refined
guarantees under strong-convexity assumptions. We also extend the
analysis to *approximate coherence*, in which the coherence constraint
is softened and incorporated into the objective
(Subsection [4.5](#S4.SS5 "4.5 Improvement guarantees for relaxed constraints ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
This setting encompasses unconstrained optimization formulations with a
relaxed notion of coherence.

Finally, we study projections with respect to a *family* of Bregman
divergences. We first show that a natural robust solution based on a
minimax objective, where the maximum is taken over a convex set of
Legendre generators, does not in general guarantee improvement
(Subsection [4.6](#S4.SS6 "4.6 Failure of Pythagorean improvement for minimax projections ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
We then consider the stronger requirement of finding a coherent Bregman
projection that is simultaneously valid for the entire family of
divergences. We provide several impossibility results for this setting,
showing that a universal improvement model can exist only under
restrictive conditions
(Subsection [4.7](#S4.SS7 "4.7 Uniform improvement guarantees and impossibility results ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).

Throughout, we sometimes work with a general closed convex set
$`\mathscr{C}`$ (not necessarily the coherent set
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$). The intersection
$`\overline{\Pi}=\mathscr{C}\cap\Pi`$, which we assume non-empty, is
then also closed and convex, since $`\Pi`$ itself is closed and convex.

For most of our analysis, we assume that the symmetry operator $`\Phi`$
is an involution. Many results, however, extend directly to the more
general case where $`\Phi`$ admits arbitrary finite orbits.

### 4.1 Bregman divergence expectation

Our analysis is often based on the expectation over $`\mathscr{X}`$ of a
Bregman divergence. Here, we prove some key properties of that
expectation, which will play an important role in several of our proofs
and analyses.

Let
$`L^{2}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}};\Omega)=\left\{\pi\colon\mathscr{X}\to\Omega\text{
measurable},\ \E\left[\left\|\pi(x)\right\|^{2}\right]<\infty\right\}`$
be the space of measurable functions from $`\mathscr{X}`$ to $`\Omega`$.
We equip this space with the $`L^{2}`$ inner product
$`\left\langle\pi,\pi^{\prime}\right\rangle=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[\left\langle\pi(x),\pi^{\prime}(x)\right\rangle]`$.

###### Lemma 7.

Let $`F\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}`$ be convex and differentiable, and
define
$`\mathsf{F}(\pi)=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[F(\pi(x))]`$,
$`\pi\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}};\Omega)`$. Then $`\mathsf{F}`$
is convex, and its Bregman divergence satisfies
$`\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{F}}(\pi\parallel\pi^{\prime})=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi(x)\parallel\pi^{\prime}(x))]`$.
If further $`F`$ is a Legendre function and bounded below, then
$`\mathsf{F}`$ is also a Legendre function on
$`L^{2}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}};\Omega)`$, with gradient
$`(\nabla\mathsf{F}(\pi))(x)=\nabla F(\pi(x))`$.

###### Proof.

Convexity of $`\mathsf{F}`$ follows immediately from the convexity of
$`F`$. The gradient of $`\mathsf{F}`$ in
$`L^{2}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}};\Omega)`$ is pointwise:

|     |                                                                    |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       (\nabla\mathsf{F}(\pi))(x)=\nabla F(\pi(x)),\quad x\in\mathscr{X},
       ```                                                                 |     |

which gives the Bregman divergence formula

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{F}}(\pi\parallel\pi^{\prime})=\mathsf{F}(\pi)-\mathsf{F}(\pi^{\prime})-\left\langle\nabla\mathsf{F}(\pi^{\prime}),\pi-\pi^{\prime}\right\rangle=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi(x)\parallel\pi^{\prime}(x))].
``` |  |

Now, assume $`F`$ is Legendre and bounded below. Since $`F`$ is proper,
closed, and differentiable with bijective gradient, consider the
constant function $`\pi_{0}(x)=p_{0}\in\dom(F)`$ with
$`F(p_{0})<+\infty`$. Then $`\mathsf{F}(\pi_{0})=F(p_{0})<+\infty`$, so
$`\mathsf{F}`$ is proper.

Let $`(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 0}`$ be a sequence in
$`L^{2}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}};\Omega)`$ converging pointwise almost
surely to $`\pi`$. Let $`m`$ be a finite lower bound of $`F`$. Applying
Fatou’s lemma to $`F-m\geq 0`$ gives

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{F}(\pi)=\E[\liminf_{n\to\infty}F(\pi_{n}(x))]\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\E[F(\pi_{n}(x))]=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{F}(\pi_{n}),
``` |  |

so $`\mathsf{F}`$ is lower semicontinuous (closed).

Finally, since $`\nabla F`$ is bijective, the pointwise mapping
$`(\nabla\mathsf{F})(\pi)(x)=\nabla F(\pi(x))`$ is a bijection on the
space of functions $`\pi\colon\mathscr{X}\to\Omega`$ in $`L^{2}`$: for
any function $`\theta\colon\mathscr{X}\to\nabla F(\Omega)`$ in
$`L^{2}`$, there exists a unique $`\pi`$ with
$`(\nabla\mathsf{F}(\pi))(x)=\theta(x)`$ for all $`x`$. Thus,
$`\mathsf{F}`$ is proper, closed, differentiable, and has bijective
gradient, and hence is Legendre on
$`L^{2}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}};\Omega)`$. ∎

The extension of this lemma to a countably infinite $`\mathscr{X}`$
requires a more detailed functional analysis, as
$`L^{2}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}};\Omega)`$ becomes an
infinite-dimensional Hilbert space. The proofs for convexity and the
form of the gradient and divergence remain valid. However, two key
properties of Legendre functions require significant additional
justification. (1) Closed property: The proof that $`\mathsf{F}`$ is
closed (lower semicontinuous) must be formalized for the $`L^{2}`$
topology. The provided proof relies on pointwise convergence. To show
$`\mathsf{F}`$ is closed in $`L^{2}`$, one must show that for any
sequence $`\pi_{n}\to\pi`$ in $`L^{2}`$,
$`\mathsf{F}(\pi)\leq\liminf\mathsf{F}(\pi_{n})`$. This can be done by
taking a subsequence $`\pi_{n_{k}}`$ that converges pointwise almost
everywhere and then applying Fatou’s lemma. (2) Bijective gradient: This
is the most critical extension. The current proof’s pointwise argument
is insufficient. One must prove that the gradient operator
$`\nabla\mathsf{F}`$ is a bijection between the Hilbert space
$`L^{2}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}};\Omega)`$ and its dual space. This
involves imposing growth conditions on $`F`$ (and its conjugate
$`F^{*}`$) to ensure that $`\pi\in L^{2}`$ implies the function
$`x\mapsto\nabla F(\pi(x))`$ is in the dual space, and conversely, that
the inverse map $`(\nabla F)^{-1}`$ also maps the dual space back to
$`L^{2}`$. This is often established using Rockafellar-type results on
integral functionals.

### 4.2 Improvement guarantees

The following result shows that Bregman projection with a closed convex
set $`\mathscr{C}`$, in particular a coherence set
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$, improves the baseline, when the optimal
policy $`\pi^{*}`$ is in $`(\mathscr{C}\cap\Pi)`$.

###### 8Convex set Bregman–projection improves the baseline.

Let $`\mathscr{C}`$ be a closed convex set. Assume that
$`\mathscr{C}\cap\Pi`$ contains the optimal conditional distribution
function $`\pi^{*}\colon\mathscr{X}\to\Delta(\mathscr{Y})`$. Then, a
solution $`\widehat{\pi}`$ of the following convex optimization problem:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\min_{\pi\in\Pi}\quad`$ | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]`$ |  | (1) |
|  | subject to | $`\displaystyle\pi\in\mathscr{C}\cap\Pi,`$ |  |  |

satisfies:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x)\right)\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]-\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right].`$ |  |

Assume that $`\Phi`$ is an involution and that $`F`$ is $`\mu`$-strongly
convex with respect to the norm $`\|\cdot\|`$. Then, the following
holds:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x)\right)\right]\leq\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]-\frac{\mu}{2}\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{X}}\left[\lambda(x)(1-\lambda(x))\left\|\pi_{0}(x)-\pi_{0}(\Phi(x))\right\|^{2}\right],
``` |  |

where $`\lambda(x)=\frac{\P(x)}{\P(x)+\P(\Phi(x))}`$.

###### Proof.

By
Lemma [7](#Thmtheorem7 "Lemma 7. ‣ 4.1 Bregman divergence expectation ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
the expectation of the Bregman divergence $`\mathsf{B}_{F}`$, denoted
$`\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{F}}`$, is a Bregman divergence associated to the
expectation of $`F`$,
$`\mathsf{F}(\pi)=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[F(\pi(x))]`$.
Thus, by definition of Bregman divergence, $`\widehat{\pi}`$ is the
$`\mathsf{F}`$-Bregman projection of $`\pi_{0}`$ on
$`\mathscr{C}\cap\Pi`$. By the Pythagorean theorem for Bregman
divergences, we can write for $`\pi^{*}\in\mathscr{C}\cap\Pi`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{F}}(\pi^{*}\parallel\pi_{0})\geq\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{F}}(\pi^{*}\parallel\widehat{\pi})+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{F}}(\widehat{\pi}\parallel\pi_{0}).
``` |  |

Thus, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))]=\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{F}}(\pi^{*}\parallel\widehat{\pi})`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{F}}(\pi^{*}\parallel\pi_{0})-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{F}}(\widehat{\pi}\parallel\pi_{0})`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))]-\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))].`$ |  |

This proves the first inequalities. For the explicit bound when
$`\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$, since $`\widehat{\pi}`$ is
coherent and $`\Phi`$ is an involution, we can write:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathscr{X}}\left[\P(x)\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))+\P(\Phi(x))\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(\Phi(x))\parallel\pi_{0}(\Phi(x)))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathscr{X}}\left[\P(x)\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))+\P(\Phi(x))\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(\Phi(x)))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathscr{X}}(\P(x)+\P(\Phi(x)))\big[\lambda(x)\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))+(1-\lambda(x))\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(\Phi(x)))\big].`$ |  |

Let the term inside the square brackets be denoted
$`W(x,\widehat{\pi})`$. By the $`\mu`$-strong convexity of $`F`$ with
respect to $`\|\cdot\|`$, we have
$`\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})\geq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\mathsf{p}-\mathsf{q}\|^{2}`$,
thus

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
W(x,\widehat{\pi})\geq\frac{\mu}{2}\left[\lambda(x)\|\widehat{\pi}(x)-\pi_{0}(x)\|^{2}+(1-\lambda(x))\|\widehat{\pi}(x)-\pi_{0}(\Phi(x))\|^{2}\right].
``` |  |

Using the identity
$`\lambda\|\mathsf{r}-\mathsf{p}\|^{2}+(1-\lambda)\|\mathsf{r}-\mathsf{q}\|^{2}=\|\mathsf{r}-(\lambda\mathsf{p}+(1-\lambda)\mathsf{q})\|^{2}+\lambda(1-\lambda)\|\mathsf{p}-\mathsf{q}\|^{2}\geq\lambda(1-\lambda)\|\mathsf{p}-\mathsf{q}\|^{2}`$,
we obtain:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
W(x,\widehat{\pi})\geq\frac{\mu}{2}\lambda(x)(1-\lambda(x))\|\pi_{0}(x)-\pi_{0}(\Phi(x))\|^{2}.
``` |  |

Replacing the term inside the square brackets with this lower bound
yields

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))]`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathscr{X}}(\P(x)+\P(\Phi(x)))\frac{\mu}{2}\lambda(x)(1-\lambda(x))\|\pi_{0}(x)-\pi_{0}(\Phi(x))\|^{2}`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{\mu}{2}\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{X}}\left[\lambda(x)(1-\lambda(x))\|\pi_{0}(x)-\pi_{0}(\Phi(x))\|^{2}\right].`$ |  |

Combining this with the main inequality yields the explicit bound stated
in the theorem. ∎

The theorem shows that the coherent conditional distribution
$`\widehat{\pi}`$ with minimal $`\mathsf{B}_{F}`$-divergence from
$`\pi_{0}`$ is closer to $`\pi^{*}`$ than $`\pi_{0}`$. In particular, if
we choose $`F`$ to be the negative entropy, and thus $`\mathsf{B}_{F}`$
the unnormalized relative entropy $`\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}`$, the
theorem guarantees that $`\widehat{\pi}`$ admits a $`\log`$-loss no
larger than $`\pi_{0}`$, since:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x)\right)\right]=\E_{(x,y)}\left[\left[-\log(\widehat{\pi}(x,y))\right]-\left[-\log(\pi^{*}(x,y))\right]\right].
``` |  |

Note also that the negative entropy is $`1`$-strongly convex with
respect to norm $`1`$ (Schützenberger-Pinsker inequality ([Rioul,
2023](#bib.bib13))), thus $`\mu=1`$ in that case.

In the special case where $`\Pi\cap\mathscr{C}`$ is an affine set, the
Pythagorean inequality in the first statement of the theorem becomes an
equality
(Theorem [20](#Thmtheorem20 "20Pythagorean Theorem equality. ‣ 5.1 Pythagorean theorem equality ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
In our setting, however, where $`\Pi`$ is bounded, this can only occur
in the trivial case where the intersection reduces to a single point (we
are assuming it is non empty).

### 4.3 Improvement guarantees in non-realizable setting

For the following result, we no longer assume that $`\pi^{*}`$ is in
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi`$.

###### 9Coherent Bregman–projection with non-realizable $`\pi^{*}`$.

Assume that $`\Phi`$ is an involution. Let
$`\epsilon=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x))\right]`$,
where $`\overline{\pi}`$ is the Bregman-projection of $`\pi^{*}`$ onto
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi`$. Then, the solution
$`\widehat{\pi}`$ of the following convex optimization problem:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\min_{\pi\in\Pi}\quad`$ | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]`$ |  |
|  | subject to | $`\displaystyle\pi\in\mathscr{C}\cap\Pi,`$ |  |

satisfies:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]+\E\left[\langle\pi^{*}(x)-\overline{\pi}(x),\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))\rangle\right].`$ |  |

If that $`F`$ is $`\mu`$-strongly convex with respect to the norm
$`\|\cdot\|`$, then, the following more explicit bound holds:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x)\right)\right]-\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq-\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]+\sqrt{\frac{2\epsilon}{\mu}}\sqrt{\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\|\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))\|_{*}^{2}\right]}.`$ |  |

Furthermore, $`F`$ is also $`L`$-smooth, then, we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]\leq-D+\frac{2L}{\mu}\sqrt{\epsilon D},
``` |  |

where
$`D=\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$.
The right-hand side is strictly negative whenever
$`\epsilon<\left[\frac{\mu}{2L}\right]^{2}D`$.

###### Proof.

By the triangle inequality-type identity for Bregman divergences, we can
write:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x))+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\overline{\pi}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))+\langle\pi^{*}(x)-\overline{\pi}(x),\nabla F(\overline{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))\rangle`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x))+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\overline{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))+\langle\pi^{*}(x)-\overline{\pi}(x),\nabla F(\overline{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x))\rangle.`$ |  |

Subtracting the second inequality from the first one and taking
expectation yields:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\overline{\pi}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\overline{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad+\langle\pi^{*}(x)-\overline{\pi}(x),\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))\rangle.`$ |  |

Now, by the Pythagorean theorem, since $`\widehat{\pi}`$ is the
projection of $`\pi_{0}`$ onto $`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi`$ and
since $`\overline{\pi}`$ is in $`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi`$ we
have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\overline{\pi}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]\leq\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\overline{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right].
``` |  |

Using this to bound the first term of right-hand side equality above
yields

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]+\E\left[\langle\pi^{*}(x)-\overline{\pi}(x),\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))\rangle\right].`$ |  |

By the strong-convexity of $`F`$, we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\epsilon=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x))\right]\geq\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\frac{\mu}{2}\|\pi^{*}(x)-\overline{\pi}(x)\|^{2}\right]
``` |  |

Thus, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]+\sqrt{\frac{2\epsilon}{\mu}}\sqrt{\E\left[\|\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))\|_{*}^{2}\right]}.`$ |  |

If $`F`$ is $`L`$-smooth, then

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E\left[\|\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))\|_{*}^{2}\right]\leq L^{2}\E\left[\|\pi_{0}(x)-\widehat{\pi}(x)\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu}\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right].
``` |  |

Define
$`D=\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$,
then, we can write:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]\leq-D+\frac{2L}{\mu}\sqrt{\epsilon D}.`$ |  |

The right-hand is negative iff $`\frac{2L}{\mu}\sqrt{\epsilon D}<D`$,
that is $`\epsilon<\left[\frac{\mu}{2L}\right]^{2}D`$. ∎

The theorem shows that when $`F`$ is $`\mu`$-strongly convex, for
$`\pi^{*}`$ approximately realizable ($`\epsilon`$ small), the coherent
projection still guarantees an improvement over the baseline
$`\pi_{0}`$. Moreover, if $`F`$ is also $`L`$-smooth, a strict
improvement is guaranteed whenever
$`\epsilon<\left[\frac{\mu}{2L}\right]^{2}D`$.

In the special case of the unnormalized relative entropy where $`F`$ is
the negative entropy, $`F`$ is $`1`$-strongly convex with respect to
$`\|\cdot\|_{1}`$, thus $`\mu=1`$. In general, however, $`F`$ is not
globally smooth, since
$`(\nabla F)_{i}(\mathsf{p})=1+\log\mathsf{p}_{i}`$ and the Hessian is
diagonal with entries $`1/p_{i}`$. Nevertheless, if we can restrict
attention to
$`\mathscr{S}_{\alpha}=\left\{\mathsf{p}\colon\mathsf{p}_{i}\geq\alpha\right\}`$,
then the Hessian operator norm is bounded by $`1/\alpha`$ and
$`\nabla F`$ is $`L`$-Lipschitz with $`L\leq 1/\alpha`$.

### 4.4 Improvement guarantees for empirical Bregman–projection

We now analyze the properties of the solution $`\widehat{\pi}_{S}`$ of
the empirical optimization problem based on an i.i.d. sample $`S`$.
Specifically, we seek to compare
$`\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S})`$ with
$`\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}\parallel\widehat{\pi})`$.

#### 4.4.1 General guarantees

This section establishes performance guarantees for the empirical
solution $`\widehat{\pi}_{S}`$ that hold in the most general case.
Crucially, these results do not rely on structural assumptions about the
objective functional, such as strong convexity. The primary result,
Theorem [10](#Thmtheorem10 "10Guarantees for empirical Bregman–projection. ‣ 4.4.1 General guarantees ‣ 4.4 Improvement guarantees for empirical Bregman–projection ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
provides a general bound applicable to any valid Bregman projection
problem.

###### 10Guarantees for empirical Bregman–projection.

Let $`\mathscr{C}`$ be a closed convex set and assume that the
intersection $`\overline{\Pi}=\mathscr{C}\cap\Pi`$ is also closed and
convex. Let $`S=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})`$ be a sample of size $`m`$ drawn
i.i.d. from $`\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}`$. Let
$`\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X}}`$ denote the corresponding
empirical distribution. Define the function class

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{F}=\left\{x\mapsto\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\colon\pi\in\overline{\Pi}\right\}\cup\left\{x\mapsto\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{\prime}(x)\parallel\pi(x))\colon\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\overline{\Pi}\right\},
``` |  |

and set
$`\epsilon_{m}=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left\lvert\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[f(x)]-\E_{x\sim\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X}}}[f(x)]\right\rvert`$.
Let $`\widehat{\pi}`$ denote a (population) minimizer of
$`\pi\mapsto\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]`$
over $`\overline{\Pi}`$ and let $`\widehat{\pi}_{S}`$ denote the
corresponding empirical minimizer of
$`\pi\mapsto\E_{x\sim\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]`$
over $`\overline{\Pi}`$. Assume that $`\pi^{*}`$ is in
$`\overline{\Pi}`$. Then, the following holds:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad+6\epsilon_{m}+\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right].`$ |  |

In particular, if $`\pi^{*}=\widehat{\pi}`$, then, the bound simplifies
to:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))\right]\leq\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]+6\epsilon_{m}.
``` |  |

###### Proof.

The proof consists of combining the empirical and population optimality
of $`\widehat{\pi}`$ and $`\widehat{\pi}_{S}`$, applying the
triangular-type Bregman identity to relate their gradients via
$`\pi^{*}`$, and bounding the resulting difference using standard
generalization inequalities. We will seek a bound on the expectation of
$`\Delta(x)=\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))`$.
By the triangular identity for Bregman divergences, for any
$`x\in\mathscr{X}`$, the following equality holds:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))\\
=\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))+\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\widehat{\pi}(x)\right\rangle.
``` |  |

In view of that, we can rewrite the expression of $`\Delta(x)`$ as
follows:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Delta(x)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))+\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\widehat{\pi}(x)\right\rangle`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))+\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\widehat{\pi}(x)\right\rangle`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)),\widehat{\pi}(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\rangle+\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\widehat{\pi}(x)\right\rangle`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))`$ |  | (identity $`\mathsf{B}_{F}(a\parallel b)+\mathsf{B}_{F}(b\parallel a)=\left\langle\nabla F(a)-\nabla F(b),a-b\right\rangle`$) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\right\rangle-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x)).`$ |  |  |

Define $`\Psi(x)`$ to be the first term on the right-hand side:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Psi(x)=\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\right\rangle.`$ |  |

We will now bound $`\E[\Psi]`$ by decomposing it into the sum of two
terms $`\E[\Psi]=(\E-\widehat{\E})[\Psi]+\widehat{\E}[\Psi]`$, where we
use the shorhand $`\widehat{\E}`$ to denote the empirical expectation.
By definition of $`\epsilon_{m}`$ and since $`\Psi`$ is a sum of three
functions in $`\mathcal{F}`$, we have
$`(\E-\widehat{\E})[\Psi]\leq 3\epsilon_{m}`$ (recall from the
derivation relating $`\Delta(x)`$ and $`\Psi(x)`$ that we can express
$`\Psi(x)`$ as
$`\Psi(x)=\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))`$).
By the optimality of $`\widehat{\pi}_{S}`$ as an empirical minimizer,
for any $`\pi\in\overline{\Pi}`$, we have,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\widehat{\E}\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\pi(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\right\rangle\right]\geq 0.
``` |  |

Thus, since $`\pi^{*}`$ is in $`\overline{\Pi}`$, we can write

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{\E}[\Psi(x)]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\widehat{\E}\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\right\rangle\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\widehat{\E}\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\right\rangle\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle-\widehat{\E}\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\right\rangle\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\widehat{\E}\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\right\rangle\right].`$ |  |

We now write the right-hand side as the difference of two terms and
apply the triangular-type Bregman identity to each:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{\E}\left[\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\rangle\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\widehat{\E}\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\pi_{0}(x)\right\rangle\right]-\widehat{\E}\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)-\pi_{0}(x)\right\rangle\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\widehat{\E}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi_{0}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad-\widehat{\E}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi_{0}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\widehat{\E}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]-\widehat{\E}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi})\right]+\widehat{\E}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right].`$ |  |

Dropping the nonpositive term
$`-\widehat{\E}\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\leq 0`$
yields

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\widehat{\E}[\Psi(x)]\leq\widehat{\E}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]+\widehat{\E}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right].
``` |  |

Combining this inequality with the one bounding the difference of
population and empirical terms, we obtain

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E[\Psi(x)]\leq 3\epsilon_{m}+\widehat{\E}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]+\widehat{\E}\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x)).
``` |  |

Replacing the empirical difference by population plus deviations gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mspace{-150mu}\widehat{\E}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+(\widehat{\E}-\E)\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]-(\widehat{\E}-\E)\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]+2\epsilon_{m},`$ |  |

since
$`\left\lvert(\widehat{\E}-\E)[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))]\right\rvert\leq\epsilon_{m}`$
for all $`\pi\in\overline{\Pi}`$. Thus, we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E[\Psi(x)]\leq 5\epsilon_{m}+\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]+\widehat{\E}\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x)).
``` |  |

In view of that, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\E[\Delta(x)]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\E[\Psi]-\E\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq 5\epsilon_{m}+\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]+\left(\widehat{\E}-\E\right)\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right].`$ |  |

The last term is bounded by $`\epsilon_{m}`$ since
$`\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi})`$ is in
$`\mathcal{F}`$. Thus,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\E[\Delta(x)]`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 6\epsilon_{m}+\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=6\epsilon_{m}+\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad+\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq 6\epsilon_{m}+\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right],`$ |  |

since $`\widehat{\pi}`$ is a minimizer and thus
$`\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]\leq 0`$.
Combining these inequalities gives

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))\right]\leq\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]+6\epsilon_{m}+\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right].
``` |  |

This completes the proof. ∎

The quantity $`\epsilon_{m}`$ is precisely the empirical process term
that is controlled by generalization bounds. In particular,
$`\epsilon_{m}`$ can be bounded (with high probability over the draw of
the sample $`S`$) in terms of the Rademacher complexity of the
considered family, the sample size $`m`$, and an upper bound on the
function values within that family (see, for example, ([Koltchinskii and
Panchenko, 2000](#bib.bib44); [Koltchinskii and Panchenko,
2002](#bib.bib45); [Bartlett and Mendelson, 2002](#bib.bib46); [Mohri et
al., 2018](#bib.bib14))).

The theorem bounds the excess divergence
$`\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi})\right]`$
by the sum of an estimation error $`6\epsilon_{m}`$ and an approximation
error
$`\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$.
The approximation term is inherent and non-negative by the Bregman
Pythagorean theorem. It can be relatively small when $`\pi^{*}`$ is
relative close to $`\pi_{0}`$ or $`\widehat{\pi}`$. Previously, we
established that
$`\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}(x))\right]\leq\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$
for $`\pi^{*}\in\overline{\Pi}`$. Thus, for a sufficiently large sample
$`m`$ so that $`\epsilon_{m}`$ is small, and a controlled approximation
error, a similar guarantee approximately holds for the empirical
minimizer $`\widehat{\pi}_{S}`$.

The factor $`6`$ appearing in the bound is due to a conservative
grouping of deviation terms. Defining $`\epsilon_{m}`$ exactly as
$`\epsilon_{m}=\sup_{\pi\in\overline{\Pi}}\left\lvert(\E-\widehat{\E})[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))]\right\rvert\vee\sup_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\overline{\Pi}}\left\lvert(\E-\widehat{\E})\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{\prime}\parallel\pi)\right]\right\rvert`$
and using more detailed calculations with tighter sign control, the same
argument as in the proof is likely to lead to a more favorable constant
factor $`2`$.

###### Corollary 11 (Guarantee for Empirical Improvement).

Under the assumptions of
Theorem [10](#Thmtheorem10 "10Guarantees for empirical Bregman–projection. ‣ 4.4.1 General guarantees ‣ 4.4 Improvement guarantees for empirical Bregman–projection ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
the following inequality holds for $`\widehat{\pi}_{S}`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{S}(x))\right]-\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]\\
\leq 6\epsilon_{m}+\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]-2\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right].
``` |  |

###### Proof.

The proof follows immediately the statement of
Theorem [10](#Thmtheorem10 "10Guarantees for empirical Bregman–projection. ‣ 4.4.1 General guarantees ‣ 4.4 Improvement guarantees for empirical Bregman–projection ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
and the Bregman Pythagorean theorem (see
Theorem [8](#Thmtheorem8 "8Convex set Bregman–projection improves the baseline. ‣ 4.2 Improvement guarantees ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
∎

The corollary offers an explicit guarantee for the performance gain
achieved by the empirical solution $`\widehat{\pi}_{S}`$ over the
initial reference $`\pi_{0}`$. It shows that the empirical solution
$`\widehat{\pi}_{S}`$ is a definite improvement over the starting point
$`\pi_{0}`$ when the inherent benefit of the projection, captured by
$`2\E[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}\parallel\pi_{0})]`$, is large enough
to overcome the statistical cost of finite-sample estimation.

#### 4.4.2 Discussion

The guarantee provided by
Theorem [10](#Thmtheorem10 "10Guarantees for empirical Bregman–projection. ‣ 4.4.1 General guarantees ‣ 4.4 Improvement guarantees for empirical Bregman–projection ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")
