````math
Lemma [7](#Thmtheorem7 "Lemma 7. ‣ 4.1 Bregman divergence expectation ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
the expectation of the Bregman divergence $`\mathsf{B}_{F}`$ is also a
Bregman divergence. Thus, since $`\pi^{*}`$ is in
$`(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi)`$, by the Pythagorean theorem, we
can write

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\right)\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x)\right)\right]-\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x)\right)\right].`$ |  | (8) |

By the Pythagorean theorem, considering the projection of $`\pi_{0}`$
onto $`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger}`$, we can also write

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x)\right)\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]-\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\overline{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right].`$ |  |

Combining these inequalities yields:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\right)\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad-\left[\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x)\right)\right]+\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\overline{\pi}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]\right].`$ |  |

This proves the first statement. We now analyze more precisely the first
term on the right-hand side of
([8](#S5.E8 "Equation 8 ‣ Proof. ‣ 5.3 Improvement guarantees via two step-projection ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
Using duality and
Lemma [3](#Thmtheorem3 "Lemma 3. ‣ 2.2.3 Centroid properties ‣ 2.2 Bregman divergences and Legendre functions ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
we can write:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x)\right)\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F^{*}}\left(\nabla F(\overline{\pi}(x))\parallel\nabla F(\pi^{*}(x))\right)\right]`$ |  | ($`F`$ Legendre and duality property) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=-\delta+\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\lambda(x)\,\mathsf{B}_{F^{*}}\left(\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x))\parallel\nabla F(\pi^{*}(x))\right)+(1-\lambda(x))\,\mathsf{B}_{F^{*}}\left(\nabla F(\pi_{0}(\Phi(x)))\parallel\nabla F(\pi^{*}(x))\right)\right]`$ |  | (Lemma [3](#Thmtheorem3 "Lemma 3. ‣ 2.2.3 Centroid properties ‣ 2.2 Bregman divergences and Legendre functions ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=-\delta+\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\lambda(x)\,\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)+(1-\lambda(x))\,\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(\Phi(x))\right)\right].`$ |  | (duality) |

We will show that the second expression coincides with
$`\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]`$.
Define $`b(x)=\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)`$
and note that $`1-\lambda(x)=\lambda(\Phi(x))`$. Then, we can write:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\lambda(x)\,\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)+(1-\lambda(x))\,\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(\Phi(x))\right)\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\lambda(x)\,b(x)+\lambda(\Phi(x))\,b(\Phi(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathscr{X}}\left[\P(x)\lambda(x)b(x)+\P(x)\lambda(\Phi(x))b(\Phi(x))+\P(\Phi(x))\lambda(x)b(x)+\P(\Phi(x))\lambda(\Phi(x))b(\Phi(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathscr{X}}\left[\P(x)\,b(x)+\P(\Phi(x))\,b(\Phi(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}[b(x)]=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right].`$ |  |

Thus, we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x)\right)\right]=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]-\delta.
``` |  |

Substituting this expression in
([8](#S5.E8 "Equation 8 ‣ Proof. ‣ 5.3 Improvement guarantees via two step-projection ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"))
yields the second statement and completes the proof. ∎

When
$`\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}=\widehat{\pi}`$,
the theorem establishes an alternative guarantee for $`\widehat{\pi}`$.
We briefly compare the improvement guarantees provided by our theorems
for $`\widehat{\pi}`$ and
$`\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}\parallel\pi_{0})-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}\parallel\widehat{\pi})`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}\parallel\pi_{0})`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}\parallel\pi_{0})-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}\parallel\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}})`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{B}_{F}(\overline{\pi}\parallel\pi_{0})+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}\parallel\overline{\pi}).`$ |  |

Since $`\widehat{\pi}`$ is in $`\Pi\cap\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$ and
thus also in $`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger}`$, by the
Pythagorean theorem applied to the projection of $`\pi_{0}`$ onto
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\dagger}`$ and $`\overline{\pi}`$ onto
$`\Pi\cap\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}\parallel\pi_{0})`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}\parallel\overline{\pi})+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\overline{\pi}\parallel\pi_{0})`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}\parallel\overline{\pi})`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}\parallel\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}})+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}\parallel\overline{\pi}).`$ |  |

Substituting the second inequality into the first yields:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}\parallel\pi_{0})`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}\parallel\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}})+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}\parallel\overline{\pi})+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\overline{\pi}\parallel\pi_{0})`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}\parallel\overline{\pi})+\mathsf{B}_{F}(\overline{\pi}\parallel\pi_{0}).`$ |  |

Thus, the improvement guarantee for $`\widehat{\pi}`$ is always more
favorable than the one for
$`\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}`$.

### 5.4 Improvement guarantees for relative entropy two-step projection

When $`F^{*}`$ is $`\mu`$-strongly convex, which implies the
$`1/\mu`$-smoothness of $`F`$, the bound in the second statement of the
theorem admits an explicit upper bound
$`-\frac{\mu}{2}\lambda(1-\lambda)\left\|\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(\Phi(x))\right\|^{2}`$.
In the following, we present an explicit upper bound in the case of
relative entropy, $`\mathsf{B}_{F}=\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}`$, in terms
of the Hellinger distance. Recall that the *Hellinger distance*
$`\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{Hell}}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})`$ between
two distributions $`\mathsf{p}`$ and $`\mathsf{q}`$ in $`\Delta_{p}`$ is
defined as

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{Hell}}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{p}\left[\sqrt{\mathsf{p}_{k}}-\sqrt{\mathsf{q}_{k}}\right]^{2}},
``` |  |

and that the squared Hellinger distance is thus defined by
$`\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{Hell}}^{2}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q})=1-\sum_{k=1}^{p}\sqrt{\mathsf{p}_{k}\mathsf{q}_{k}}`$.
Both distances take values in $`[0,1]`$.

###### Corollary 25.

Let $`F`$ be the negative entropy, so that
$`\mathsf{B}_{F}=\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}`$ is the (unnormalized)
relative entropy. Then, under the assumptions of
Theorem [24](#Thmtheorem24 "24Coherent two-step Bregman–projection improves the baseline. ‣ 5.3 Improvement guarantees via two step-projection ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
the $`\log`$-loss improvement of
$`\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}`$
over the baseline $`\pi_{0}`$ is at least

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[2\min\left\{\lambda(x),1-\lambda(x)\right\}\,\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{Hell}}^{2}(\pi_{0}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(\Phi(x)))\right],
``` |  |

where $`\lambda(x)=\frac{\P[x]}{\P[x]+\P[\Phi(x)]}`$.

###### Proof.

Let $`\mathsf{X}=\pi_{0}(x)`$ and $`\mathsf{Y}=\pi_{0}(\Phi(x))`$, and
define $`u_{0}=\nabla F(\mathsf{X})`$ and $`u_{1}=\nabla F(\mathsf{Y})`$
where $`F\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}`$ is the negative entropy
function defined by
$`F(\mathsf{p})=\sum_{k}\mathsf{p}_{k}\log\mathsf{p}_{k}`$, for all
$`\mathsf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}`$. $`F`$ is differentiable over
$`\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}`$. We first derive a more explicit expression for

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Delta=F^{*}(\lambda(x)u_{0}+(1-\lambda(x))u_{1})-\left[\lambda(x)F^{*}(u_{0})+(1-\lambda(x))F^{*}(u_{1})\right].
``` |  |

For any $`\mathsf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}`$, $`\nabla F(\mathsf{p})`$
is the vector of coordinates $`\log\mathsf{p}_{k}+1`$. The conjugate
function $`F^{*}`$ of $`F`$ is known to be defined by
$`F^{*}(y)=\sum_{k=1}^{d}e^{y_{k}-1}`$ for all $`y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$.
Thus, for any $`\mathsf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}`$, we have
$`F^{*}(\nabla F(\mathsf{p}))=\sum_{k}\mathsf{p}_{k}`$. In light of
that, we have
$`\left[\lambda(x)F^{*}(u_{0})+(1-\lambda(x))F^{*}(u_{1})\right]=1`$ and

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Delta=F^{*}(\lambda(x)u_{0}+(1-\lambda(x))u_{1})-1=\sum_{k=1}^{d}e^{\lambda(x)(\log\mathsf{X}_{k}+1)+(1-\lambda(x))(\log\mathsf{Y}_{k}+1)-1}-1=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathsf{X}_{k}^{\lambda}(x)\,\mathsf{Y}_{k}^{1-\lambda(x)}-1.`$ |  |

We can express $`\Delta`$ in terms of the $`\lambda(x)`$-Rényi
divergence, leverage the fact that the Rényi divergence is
non-decreasing in $`\lambda(x)`$ and the identity
$`\mathsf{D}_{1/2}(\mathsf{X}\parallel\mathsf{Y})=-2\log\left[1-\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{Hell}}^{2}(\mathsf{X}\parallel\mathsf{Y})\right]`$
([van Erven and Harremoës, 2014](#bib.bib11)) to derive the following
upper bound for $`\lambda(x)\leq\frac{1}{2}`$:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Delta`$ | $`\displaystyle=\exp((\lambda(x)-1)\mathsf{D}_{\lambda(x)}(\mathsf{X}\parallel\mathsf{Y}))-1`$ |  | (definition of $`\lambda(x)`$-Rényi divergence) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\exp((\lambda(x)-1)\mathsf{D}_{1/2}(\mathsf{X}\parallel\mathsf{Y}))-1`$ |  | ($`\lambda(x)`$-Rényi divergence non-decreasing function of $`\lambda(x)`$) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\left[1-\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{Hell}}^{2}(\mathsf{X}\parallel\mathsf{Y})\right]^{2(1-\lambda(x))}-1`$ |  | (expression of $`\mathsf{D}_{1/2}`$ in terms of Hellinger distance) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\left[1-2(1-\lambda(x))\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{Hell}}^{2}(\mathsf{X}\parallel\mathsf{Y})\right]-1`$ |  | (inequality $`(1-x)^{\alpha}\leq 1-\alpha x`$, valid for $`\alpha\in[0,1]`$ and $`x\in[0,1]`$) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=-2(1-\lambda(x))\,\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{Hell}}^{2}(\mathsf{X}\parallel\mathsf{Y}).`$ |  |  |

A symmetric argument for $`\lambda(x)\geq\frac{1}{2}`$ yields
$`\Delta\leq-2\lambda(x)\,\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{Hell}}^{2}(\mathsf{X}\parallel\mathsf{Y})`$
by first expressing $`\Delta`$ as

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\exp(\lambda(x)\mathsf{D}_{1-\lambda(x)}(\mathsf{Y}\parallel\mathsf{X}))-1.
``` |  |

Hence, in all cases, we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Delta\leq-2\min\left\{\lambda(x),1-\lambda(x)\right\}\,\mathsf{D}_{\mathrm{Hell}}^{2}(\mathsf{X}\parallel\mathsf{Y}).
``` |  |

The claim then follows by integrating this bound in
Theorem [24](#Thmtheorem24 "24Coherent two-step Bregman–projection improves the baseline. ‣ 5.3 Improvement guarantees via two step-projection ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement").
∎

Tighter inequalities with more favorable constants can be derived in
terms of the Hellinger distance at the expense of more complex proofs,
or by resorting to a refined Young’s inequality such as that of
[Kittaneh and Manasrah (2010)](#bib.bib12). Alternative bounds can also
be derived in terms of the total variation, using the inequality
$`\mathsf{D}_{\lambda(x)}(\mathsf{X}\parallel\mathsf{Y})\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}V^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})`$,
valid for all $`\lambda(x)\in(0,1]`$ ([van Erven and Harremoës,
2014](#bib.bib11)), where $`V(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})`$ is the total
variation of $`\mathsf{X}`$ and $`\mathsf{Y}`$.

### 5.5 Maximin properties

In the previous sections, we considered a fixed reference (or ideal)
coherent conditional distribution function $`\pi^{*}`$ and showed that
both the direct and two-step Bregman projection solutions yield
improvements over the baseline $`\pi_{0}`$. For a given conditional
distribution function $`\pi`$, the improvement for a reference
$`\pi^{*}`$ is measured by

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Improv_{\pi^{*}}(\pi)=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x)\right)\right]-\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}\left(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi(x)\right)\right].
``` |  |

We now turn to the problem of finding a solution that maximizes the
worst-case improvement over the choice of the reference distribution
$`\pi^{*}`$. This leads to the following max–min optimization problem:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\sup_{\pi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi}\,\inf_{\pi^{*}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi}\Improv_{\pi^{*}}(\pi).`$ |  | (9) |

When $`\mathsf{B}_{F}`$ is jointly convex, as in the squared distance
and the relative entropy, $`\Improv_{\pi^{*}}(\pi)`$ is a concave
function of $`\pi`$, since the infimum of a set of concave functions is
also concave. Since $`\Pi`$ is a (closed) convex set,
([9](#S5.E9 "Equation 9 ‣ 5.5 Maximin properties ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"))
is then a convex optimization in $`\pi`$. Our next result shows that the
two-step-Bregman projection
$`\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}`$ is
always a solution to this problem.

###### 26.

Let $`\Phi\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{X}`$ be an involution and let
$`\Pi`$ be a convex and closed family of conditional distribution
functions $`\pi\colon\mathscr{X}\to\Delta(\mathscr{Y})`$. Assume that
$`F`$ is a Legendre function. Then, the two-step-Bregman projection is a
solution of
Problem [9](#S5.E9 "Equation 9 ‣ 5.5 Maximin properties ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement").

###### Proof.

Since both $`\pi^{*}`$ and $`\pi`$ are in
$`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$, we can write:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Improv_{\pi^{*}}(\pi)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi(x))\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathscr{X}}\Big[\P(x)\,\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))+\P(\Phi(x))\,\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(\Phi(x)))`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad-(\P(x)+\P(\Phi(x)))\,\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi(x))\Big]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathscr{X}}(\P(x)+\P(\Phi(x)))\,\Big[\lambda(x)\,\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))+(1-\lambda(x))\,\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(\Phi(x)))`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi(x))\Big]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\Big[\lambda(x)\,\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))+(1-\lambda(x))\,\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(\Phi(x)))-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi(x))\Big],`$ |  |

where we adopt the notation used in
Theorem [24](#Thmtheorem24 "24Coherent two-step Bregman–projection improves the baseline. ‣ 5.3 Improvement guarantees via two step-projection ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"):
$`\lambda(x)=\frac{\P(x)}{\P(x)+\P(\Phi(x))}`$. Using
Lemma [3](#Thmtheorem3 "Lemma 3. ‣ 2.2.3 Centroid properties ‣ 2.2 Bregman divergences and Legendre functions ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
we can rewrite the sum of the first two terms within the expectation as
follows:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\lambda(x)\,\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))+(1-\lambda(x))\,\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(\Phi(x)))`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\lambda(x)\,\mathsf{B}_{F^{*}}(\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x))\parallel\nabla F(\pi^{*}(x)))+(1-\lambda(x))\,\mathsf{B}_{F^{*}}(\nabla F(\pi_{0}(\Phi(x)))\parallel\nabla F(\pi^{*}(x)))`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x))+\delta(x),`$ |  |

where
$`\delta(x)=\left[\lambda(x)F^{*}(u_{0})+(1-\lambda(x))F^{*}(u_{1})\right]-F^{*}(\lambda(x)u_{0}+(1-\lambda(x))u_{1})\geq 0`$,
with $`u_{0}=\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x))`$ and
$`u_{1}=\nabla F(\pi_{0}(\Phi(x)))`$, and

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\overline{\pi}(x)=(\nabla F)^{-1}\left(\lambda(x)\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x))+(1-\lambda(x))\nabla F(\pi_{0}(\Phi(x)))\right).
``` |  |

It is straightforward to check that $`\overline{\pi}`$ is coherent.
Thus, we have

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\sup_{\pi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi}\inf_{\pi^{*}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi}\Improv_{\pi^{*}}(\pi)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\sup_{\pi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi}\inf_{\pi^{*}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi}\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\big[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x))+\delta(x)-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi(x))\big]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\pi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi}\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\big[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x)))+\delta(x)-\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\parallel\pi(x))\big]`$ |  | ($`\pi^{*}`$ can be chosen to be $`\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}`$ since $`\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi`$) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\parallel\overline{\pi}(x)))+\delta(x)\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\Improv_{\pi^{*}}(\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}),`$ |  | (Theorem [24](#Thmtheorem24 "24Coherent two-step Bregman–projection improves the baseline. ‣ 5.3 Improvement guarantees via two step-projection ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")) |

for any $`\pi^{*}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}\cap\Pi`$. This shows that
$`\Improv_{\pi^{*}}(\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}})`$
attains the value of
Problem [9](#S5.E9 "Equation 9 ‣ 5.5 Maximin properties ‣ 5 Two-Step Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")
and that
$`\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}`$ is
a solution. ∎

This establishes an additional favorable property for the two-step
projection solution
$`\widehat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\widehat{\pi}}}`$
and thus also for the single-step projection $`\widehat{\pi}`$ when the
two coincide, for Bregman divergences whose generator is defined over
the full positive orthant.

## 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms

In this section, we study mechanisms that improve a baseline function
$`\pi_{0}\in\Pi`$ under Bregman divergences. We begin with the case of a
fixed divergence, showing that any mechanism guaranteeing improvement
must coincide with a Bregman projection onto a block-constant set
(Subsection [6.1](#S6.SS1 "6.1 Characterization for a single divergence ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).
We then turn to the stronger requirement of improvement across *all*
Legendre Bregman divergences. This leads to a rigidity result: the
outputs of any such mechanism are forced to lie in a *universal
block-constant structure*, independent of the particular divergence
(Subsection [6.2](#S6.SS2 "6.2 Rigidity under all divergences – Topological proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).

### 6.1 Characterization for a single divergence

We begin by analyzing mechanisms that guarantee improvement with respect
to a fixed Legendre Bregman divergence. This case serves as a building
block for understanding the universal rigidity phenomenon we analyze
later.

###### 27General improvement implies Bregman projection.

Let $`F`$ be a Legendre function and let
$`\pi_{0}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$. Suppose a mechanism produces
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\Pi`$ such that for every $`\pi^{*}\in\Pi`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x))\right]\leq\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right].
``` |  |

Assume $`\Pi`$ is closed and convex and that a.e. the relevant values
lie in the interior of the domain of $`F`$. Define the partition
$`\left\{\mathscr{X}_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}`$ by the equivalence relation
$`x\sim x^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)=\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x^{\prime})`$
and set

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathscr{C}_{F}=\left\{\pi\in\Pi\colon\pi(x)=\pi(x^{\prime}),\forall x,x^{\prime}\in\mathscr{X}_{i},\ \forall i\in I\right\}.
``` |  |

Then

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\widehat{\pi}_{F}=\argmin_{\pi\in\Pi\cap\mathscr{C}_{F}}\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right].
``` |  |

That is, $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is the (unique) Bregman projection of
$`\pi_{0}`$ onto $`\Pi\cap\mathscr{C}_{F}`$.

###### Proof.

Fix any comparator $`\pi\in\Pi\cap\mathscr{C}_{F}`$. Consider the global
convex combination (valid because $`\Pi`$ is convex)
$`\pi_{t}=(1-t)\widehat{\pi}_{F}+t\pi`$, $`t\in[0,1]`$. Define the
affine test function $`g`$ as follows:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
g(t)=\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi_{t}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi_{t}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]+\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right].
``` |  |

In the definition of $`g`$ due to the difference of the first Bregman
divergences, the terms in $`F(\pi_{t}(x))`$ vanish. As a result, $`g`$
is an affine function of $`t`$. By the assumed strong improvement
property we have $`g(t)\leq 0`$ for every $`t\in[0,1]`$. Since
$`\pi_{0}`$ and $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ are fixed, we can write

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
g(0)=\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F}\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{F})\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F}\parallel\pi_{0})\right]+\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F}\parallel\pi_{0})\right]=0.
``` |  |

Thus, $`g`$ is affine in $`t`$ and satisfies $`g(t)\leq 0`$ for all
$`t\in[0,1]`$ with $`g(0)=0`$. Its right-hand derivative at $`0`$
therefore satisfies $`g^{\prime}(0^{+})\leq 0`$. For any $`t\in(0,1)`$,
we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle g^{\prime}(t)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\Big[\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi_{t}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi_{t}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]\Big]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\Big[\E\big[F(\pi_{0}(x))-F(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x))\big]+\E\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\pi_{t}(x)-\pi_{0}(x)\right\rangle\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad-\E\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)),\pi_{t}(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\right\rangle\right]\Big]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\Big[\E\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\pi_{t}(x)-\pi_{0}(x)\right\rangle\right]-\E\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)),\pi_{t}(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\right\rangle\right]\Big]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=-\E\Big[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\ \pi(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\right\rangle\Big].`$ |  |

Thus $`g^{\prime}(0^{+})\leq 0`$ implies

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\ \pi(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\right\rangle\right]\geq 0.
``` |  |

Since this holds for every $`\pi\in\Pi\cap\mathscr{C}_{F}`$, it is
exactly the first-order optimality (variational) condition for
minimizing the strictly convex functional
$`\pi\mapsto\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi\parallel\pi_{0})\right]`$ over
the closed convex set $`\Pi\cap\mathscr{C}_{F}`$. Uniqueness of the
minimizer follows the fact that $`F`$ is Legendre. Thus,
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ equals that minimizer, that is, it is the Bregman
projection of $`\pi_{0}`$ onto $`\Pi\cap\mathscr{C}_{F}`$. ∎

If the equivalence classes $`\mathscr{X}_{i}`$ are singletons or arise
as orbits of a function $`\Phi`$ (e.g., an involution) with
$`\pi(x)=\pi(\Phi(x))`$, then $`\mathscr{C}_{F}`$ coincides with the
generalized coherence set $`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$. In this case,
the mechanism output $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is exactly the Bregman
projection onto $`\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}`$.

The theorem’s requirement may appear too stringent: not only does it
require the mechanism $`\mathcal{M}`$ to improve upon the baseline
$`\pi_{0}`$ for any reference distribution $`\pi^{*}`$ but it also
requires that improvement to be at least
$`\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]`$.
However, as shown by
Theorem [8](#Thmtheorem8 "8Convex set Bregman–projection improves the baseline. ‣ 4.2 Improvement guarantees ‣ 4 Direct Coherence Projection ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
that improvement is already guaranteed by a Bregman divergence
projection over a convex set. In light of that result, the requirement
is very natural.

Although the proof of
Theorem [27](#Thmtheorem27 "27General improvement implies Bregman projection. ‣ 6.1 Characterization for a single divergence ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")
relies on standard variational arguments, the theorem itself is far from
trivial. The key insight is that the strong universal improvement
condition, requiring the mechanism to reduce the Bregman divergence
relative to *all* comparators $`\pi^{*}\in\Pi`$, forces the output
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ to lie in a highly structured subset of $`\Pi`$.
Specifically, the equivalence-class construction defining
$`\mathscr{C}_{F}`$ is not imposed a priori but emerges naturally from
the mechanism’s output: any two inputs that the mechanism maps to the
same value define a class, and the mechanism’s output must be constant
across each class. This structure is remarkable because it reveals a
rigidity phenomenon: a mechanism cannot achieve universal improvement
without collapsing its output onto a convex set
$`\Pi\cap\mathscr{C}_{F}`$ that respects these equivalence classes. In
other words, the theorem shows that universal improvement does not
merely constrain the value of $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$, but also forces a
specific piecewise-constant geometry on its output, which is a
nontrivial structural characterization.

### 6.2 Rigidity under all divergences – Topological proof

In mathematics, rigidity theorems show that certain structures,
particularly in geometry and topology, are resisting to perturbation
([Mostow, 1973](#bib.bib17); [Gromov and Pansu, 1991](#bib.bib15);
[Spatzier, 2004](#bib.bib16)). We find an analogous principle in
self-improvement, where a mechanism must improve an initial policy
$`\pi_{0}`$ simultaneously across all Legendre Bregman divergences.
While the optimal solution $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ can vary with each
divergence $`F`$, this demand for universal improvement imposes a
powerful structural constraint. This is the rigidity we identify: all
possible outputs are confined to the same block-constant set, as with
the coherent set.

This result is stronger than the single-$`F`$ case. For a fixed Legendre
function $`F`$, the mechanism output $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is a Bregman
projection onto a set $`\mathscr{C}_{F}`$ that can depend on the output
itself. In contrast, the demand for universal improvement forces all
outputs to project onto a single, common set $`\mathscr{C}`$. This
shared constraint is what forges the rigid, block-constant structure
that any such mechanism must invariably produce, regardless of the
specific divergence $`F`$.

The proof of rigidity for general families of divergences is far from
trivial. At first sight, one might expect the argument to proceed by
establishing a general variational inequality for the mechanism and then
intersecting the coherence sets $`\mathscr{C}_{F}`$ across all
generators $`F`$. However, this approach quickly runs into trouble: even
for two functions, the intersection
$`\mathscr{C}_{F_{1}}\cap\mathscr{C}_{F_{2}}`$ need not be nontrivial.
The real challenge is not to characterize the intersection of partitions
but to prove that the partitions themselves are identical. Continuity of
the map $`F\mapsto\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is an important ingredient, yet it
does not by itself resolve this issue. A critical insight is that the
rigidity proof may require fundamentally different strategies depending
on the geometry of the constraint set $`\Pi`$, making a general theorem
substantially more delicate.

In this section, we prove a rigidity theorem using a topological
argument. We will assume stability, that is the continuity of the output
map $`F\mapsto\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$. We will further assume a strictness
assumption, which provides a safety margin in the optimality condition.
Together, surprisingly, these assumptions allow us to prove that the
solution’s coherence set $`\mathscr{C}_{F}`$ is *sticky*, forcing every
generator in a connected family to share the same universal coherence
set.

###### 28Topological rigidity.

Let $`\mathcal{F}`$ be a family of Legendre functions whose domains
contain a fixed open convex neighborhood of $`\Delta(\mathscr{Y})`$. Let
$`\Pi\subset\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$ be closed and convex and let
$`\pi_{0}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$. Let $`\mathcal{M}`$ be a mechanism
that for every $`F\in\mathcal{F}`$ produces an output
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\Pi`$ satisfying the strong improvement
inequality: for all $`\pi^{*}\in\Pi`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x))\right]\leq\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right].
``` |  |

Assume that $`F\mapsto\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is continuous from the
generator topology (uniform convergence of $`F`$ and $`\nabla F`$ on
$`\Delta(\mathscr{Y})`$) into $`L^{1}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}})`$
(stability).

Define the partition $`\left\{\mathscr{X}_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}`$ by the
equivalence relation
$`x\sim x^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}}(x)=\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}}(x^{\prime})`$
(using the level sets of a chosen seed $`F_{0}\in\mathcal{F}`$) and set
$`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}=\left\{\pi\in\Pi\colon\pi(x)=\pi(x^{\prime}),\forall x,x^{\prime}\in\mathscr{X}_{i},\ \forall i\in I\right\}`$.
Assume further *strictness* on the path-connected family
$`\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}`$ containing $`F_{0}`$:
there exists $`\gamma>0`$ such that for every
$`F\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\inf_{\pi\in\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}}\E\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\ \pi(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\right\rangle\right]\geq\gamma.
``` |  |

Then, for every $`F\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}`$ the mechanism output
satisfies $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ a.e. In
particular, the partition of $`\mathscr{X}`$ induced by
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is identical for all
$`F\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}`$.

Intuitively, the theorem’s topological argument relies on the
*strictness* assumption to create a "potential well" around the
universal coherence set $`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$. The variational
inequality, which is derived from the improvement guarantee, can be
thought of as measuring the slope of a loss function ($`\Psi_{F}`$ in
the proof) at the solution $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$. The standard
optimality condition (with a $`\geq 0`$ bound) would just mean that
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is at a minimal point within the set
$`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ (the floor of the well). Any step from
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ to another $`\pi\in\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ is flat
or uphill. The strictness condition,
$`\inf_{\pi\in\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}}\Psi_{F}(\pi)\geq\gamma>0`$,
is much stronger. It means that for *any* point $`\pi`$ *outside* the
set $`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ ("cliffs" of the well), the slope in that
direction is not just non-negative, but is strictly positive and
uniformly bounded by $`\gamma`$. This $`\gamma`$-gap ensures that the
set $`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ is robustly optimal, with no other
competitors just outside the set that are nearly as good. The proof
leverages this $`\gamma`$-gap. The stability assumption guarantees that
as we perturb $`F`$, the solution $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ can only move
slightly. The $`\gamma`$-gap ensures this slight move is not enough to
jump out of the potential well. This makes the coherence set
$`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ "sticky", forcing all solutions along the
continuous path $`\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}`$ to remain trapped inside it.

###### Proof.

Define
$`S=\left\{F\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\,\colon\,\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}\right\}`$.
We show that $`S`$ is nonempty, open and closed in
$`\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}`$, which by path-connectedness implies
$`S=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}`$. $`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ is considered
modulo null sets, thus, throughout the proof, the qualifier ‘a.e.’ will
be understood implicitly in the relevant statements, and thus will not
be repeated.

By assumption, $`F_{0}`$ is in $`\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}`$ and
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}}\in\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$, so $`S`$ contains
$`F_{0}`$ and is non-empty. Let $`(F_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset S`$ with
$`F_{n}\to F_{\infty}`$. By stability, we also have
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F_{n}}\to\widehat{\pi}_{F_{\infty}}`$ in
$`L^{1}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}})`$. Each $`\widehat{\pi}_{F_{n}}`$ is
in $`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ and thus blockwise constant on the fixed
partition defining $`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$. The set
$`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ is closed in
$`L^{1}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}})`$, so the limit
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F_{\infty}}\in\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$. Hence
$`F_{\infty}\in S`$, and $`S`$ is closed.

Fix any $`G\in S`$. We will define a neighborhood $`\mathscr{U}`$ of
$`G`$ contained in $`S`$. To do so, we show that the strictness property
of $`G`$ extends to a neighborhood $`\mathscr{U}`$. This disallows any
$`F\in\mathscr{U}`$ from having an output $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ outside
$`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$, as its functional
$`\Psi_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F})`$ must be zero by definition, yet the
strictness property requires it to be strictly positive. By stability,
the map $`F\mapsto\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is continuous from the generator
topology (uniform convergence of $`F`$ and $`\nabla F`$ on
$`\Delta(\mathscr{Y})`$) to $`L^{1}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}})`$.
Define, for any $`F\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}`$ and any $`\pi\in\Pi`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Psi_{F}(\pi)=\E\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\ \pi(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\right\rangle\right].
``` |  |

The map $`(F,\pi)\mapsto\Psi_{F}(\pi)`$ is continuous since
$`F\mapsto\nabla F`$ is continuous in the topology and
$`F\mapsto\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is continuous by stability. This implies
that the map $`F\mapsto\sup_{\pi\in\Pi}|\Psi_{F}(\pi)-\Psi_{G}(\pi)|`$
is also continuous.

![Refer to caption](2511.08440v1/topological.png)

Figure 2: Illustration of the proof of
Theorem [28](#Thmtheorem28 "28Topological rigidity. ‣ 6.2 Rigidity under all divergences – Topological proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement").

Therefore, we can choose a neighborhood $`\mathscr{U}`$ of $`G`$ small
enough so that for every $`F\in\mathscr{U}`$, since $`\pi`$ and
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ take values in the simplex and therefore
$`\left\lvert\pi-\widehat{\pi}_{F}\right\rvert`$ is uniformly bounded:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sup_{\pi\in\Pi}\left\lvert\Psi_{F}(\pi)-\Psi_{G}(\pi)\right\rvert<\gamma/3.
``` |  |

Since $`G`$ is in $`S`$, by the strictness hypothesis for all
$`\pi\in\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$, we have
$`\Psi_{G}(\pi)\geq\gamma`$. Combining these inequalities using the
triangle inequality, for all $`F\in\mathscr{U}`$ and all
$`\pi\in\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$, we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Psi_{F}(\pi)=\Psi_{G}(\pi)+(\Psi_{F}(\pi)-\Psi_{G}(\pi))\geq\gamma-|\Psi_{F}(\pi)-\Psi_{G}(\pi)|\geq\gamma-\gamma/3=2\gamma/3.
``` |  |

This establishes that the strictness property holds (with a smaller gap
$`2\gamma/3`$) for the entire neighborhood $`\mathscr{U}`$. Now, suppose
for contradiction that there exists some $`F\in\mathscr{U}`$ such that
its output is not in the set:
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\not\in\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$. Since
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$, we can use this
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ as the competitor $`\pi`$ in the strictness bound
we just derived: $`\Psi_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F})\geq 2\gamma/3>0`$.
However, by definition of $`\Psi_{F}(\cdot)`$, evaluating it at its own
solution $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ yields:
$`\Psi_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F})=0`$. This gives the final contradiction
$`0\geq 2\gamma/3>0`$. Thus, no such $`F`$ exists, and for every
$`F\in\mathscr{U}`$, we have
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$. This proves that
$`\mathscr{U}\subseteq S`$, and that $`S`$ is open.

Since $`S=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}`$, for every
$`F\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}`$ we have
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$. This completes the proof. ∎

We now comment on the uniform strictness assumption adopted in the
theorem. One might hope that a weaker, pointwise strictness assumption
(i.e., $`\Psi(\pi)>0`$ for all
$`\pi\in\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$) would be sufficient,
especially if $`\Pi`$ is compact. This is not the case. The set of
competitors $`\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ is not compact: it is a
compact set minus a closed subset, which is relatively open. A
continuous function on a non-compact set is not guaranteed to attain its
minimum, and its infimum can be zero even if the function is strictly
positive everywhere. For example, if $`\Pi=[-1,1]`$ and
$`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}=\left\{0\right\}`$, the functional
$`\Psi(\pi)=\pi^{2}`$ is strictly positive on
$`\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$, yet $`\inf\Psi(\pi)=0`$. Thus, the
uniform $`\gamma`$-gap is a necessary and stronger condition.

This uniform $`\gamma`$-gap holds for some important classes of convex
sets but fails for others. The assumption is likely to hold for
polyhedral sets, such as the probability simplex. In that case, $`\Pi`$
is composed of flat "faces." A competitor
$`\pi\notin\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ must lie on a different face (or be
non-aligned on the same face). This discrete, piecewise-linear geometry
can bound the functional $`\Psi(\pi)`$ away from zero, creating the
required $`\gamma`$-gap. Conversely, the assumption often fails for
smoothly curved sets. In examples with the $`L^{2}`$-ball, the
functional $`\Psi(\pi)`$ can be strictly positive for all competitors,
yet its infimum can still be zero, as the value is approached on the
boundary of $`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$.

A promising alternative proof is a topological two-path argument. First,
we establish a baseline coherence set $`\mathscr{C}_{0}`$ using a seed
generator $`F_{0}`$. For any other generator $`G`$, we define a
continuous path of generators $`F_{t}`$ connecting $`F_{0}`$ to $`G`$.
The proof then compares two continuous solution paths: (1) the "true"
path $`\widehat{\pi}_{t}=P_{\Pi}^{F_{t}}(\pi_{0})`$, which is continuous
by the stability assumption, and (2) the candidate path
$`\pi_{t}^{*}=P_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}^{F_{t}}(\pi_{0})`$, the projection
onto the fixed set $`\mathscr{C}_{0}`$. Since both paths start at the
same point ($`\widehat{\pi}_{0}=\pi_{0}^{*}`$), the proof’s objective is
to show they are identical ($`\widehat{\pi}_{t}=\pi_{t}^{*}`$ for all
$`t`$), which would imply $`\widehat{\pi}_{G}\in\mathscr{C}_{0}`$. This
requires showing that the candidate path $`\pi_{t}^{*}`$ also satisfies
the global variational inequality, which is typically guaranteed by a
strictness assumption that creates a potential well, forcing the true
path to *stick* to the set $`\mathscr{C}_{0}`$. However, perhaps
alternative techniques can be used to enforce that with weaker
assumptions.

We assume stability, that is, the continuity of the map
$`F\mapsto\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ (from the $`C^{1}`$ generator topology to
$`L^{1}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}})`$). This is a standard regularity
assumption in parametric optimization; continuity of the unique
minimizer $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is expected since it minimizes a
strictly convex functional,
$`\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\cdot\parallel\pi_{0})\right]`$, which varies
continuously with $`F`$ in the $`C^{1}`$ topology over the compact set
$`\Pi`$ (see for example Berge’s Maximum Theorem ([Berge,
1963](#bib.bib77); [Aliprantis and Border, 2006](#bib.bib78))).
Rigorously verifying the conditions for continuity in this specific
functional setting is left for future work.

#### 6.2.1 Toy example

This simple example is convenient to illustrate the rigidity theorem.
The example is first constructed so that $`\Pi`$ enforces a
block-equality constraint. The coherence set $`\mathscr{C}`$ (derived
from any generator) then coincides with $`\Pi`$, and the strictness
condition holds *vacuously*, as $`\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}`$ is empty. We
then show how strictness *fails* in a non-trivial case.

Let $`\mathscr{X}=\left\{1,2,3\right\}`$ and
$`\mathscr{Y}=\left\{0,1\right\}`$. We identify a conditional model
$`\pi`$ with the vector $`p=(p(1),p(2),p(3))`$ where
$`p(x)=\Pr[y=1\mid x]`$. Let $`\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}`$ be uniform on
$`\mathscr{X}`$ (weight $`1/3`$ each). Fix the baseline $`\pi_{0}`$ with

|     |                                                          |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       p_{0}(1)=0.10,\qquad p_{0}(2)=0.80,\qquad p_{0}(3)=0.40.
       ```                                                       |     |

Define the feasible set $`\Pi`$ by the single block constraint that
$`p(1)=p(2)`$, that is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Pi=\left\{\pi=(p(1),p(2),p(3))\colon p(1)=p(2),\ p(i)\in[0,1]\right\}.
``` |  |

The corresponding block partition is
$`\mathscr{X}_{1}=\left\{1,2\right\}`$ and
$`\mathscr{X}_{2}=\left\{3\right\}`$. The block-constant coherence set
defined relative to $`\Pi`$ is

|     |                                                         |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathscr{C}=\left\{\pi\in\Pi:\pi(1)=\pi(2)\right\}=\Pi.
       ```                                                      |     |

In this case, $`\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}`$ is the empty set, so the
infimum in the strictness condition is taken over an empty set (and is
$`+\infty`$), meaning the condition is vacuously satisfied. As predicted
by
Theorem [28](#Thmtheorem28 "28Topological rigidity. ‣ 6.2 Rigidity under all divergences – Topological proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
the projection $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ must lie in $`\mathscr{C}`$ for any
$`F`$. We verify this for several generators.

The projection $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ of $`\pi_{0}`$ onto $`\Pi`$
minimizes the expected Bregman divergence. Since the constraint only
links $`p(1)`$ and $`p(2)`$, the minimizer $`p(3)`$ is always
$`p_{0}(3)=0.40`$. The value $`q^{\star}=p(1)=p(2)`$ minimizes
$`\mathsf{B}_{F}(q\parallel p_{0}(1))+\mathsf{B}_{F}(q\parallel p_{0}(2))`$.
By
Lemma [4](#Thmtheorem4 "Lemma 4. ‣ 2.2.3 Centroid properties ‣ 2.2 Bregman divergences and Legendre functions ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
$`q^{\star}`$ is the Bregman centroid, satisfying
$`\nabla F(q^{\star})=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla F(p_{0}(1))+\nabla F(p_{0}(2)))`$.

Squared-Euclidean generator. Take
$`F_{\mathrm{sq}}(p)=\tfrac{1}{2}\left\|p\right\|^{2}`$. Then
$`\nabla F(p)=p`$. The centroid is the arithmetic mean:

|     |                                                                    |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       q^{\star}=\frac{1}{2}(p_{0}(1)+p_{0}(2))=\frac{0.10+0.80}{2}=0.45.
       ```                                                                 |     |

Hence

|     |                                                             |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{sq}}=(0.45,0.45,0.40)\in\mathscr{C}.
       ```                                                          |     |

Negative-entropy generator ($`F(p)=p\log p`$). Here
$`\nabla F(p)=\log p+1`$. The centroid $`q^{\star}`$ satisfies:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\log q^{\star}+1`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[(\log p_{0}(1)+1)+(\log p_{0}(2)+1)]`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\log q^{\star}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[\log(0.1)+\log(0.8)]=\log\sqrt{0.08}`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle q^{\star}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\sqrt{0.08}\approx 0.2828.`$ |  |

Thus

|     |                                                                 |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{KL}}=(0.2828,0.2828,0.40)\in\mathscr{C}.
       ```                                                              |     |

Negative logarithm generator ($`F(p)=-\log p`$). Here
$`\nabla F(p)=-1/p`$. The centroid $`q^{\star}`$ (the harmonic mean)
satisfies:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle-1/q^{\star}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[-1/p_{0}(1)-1/p_{0}(2)]`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle 1/q^{\star}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[1/0.1+1/0.8]=\frac{1}{2}[10+1.25]=5.625`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle q^{\star}`$ | $`\displaystyle=1/5.625=8/45\approx 0.1778.`$ |  |

Thus

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{Itakura-Saito}}=(0.1778,0.1778,0.40)\in\mathscr{C}.
``` |  |

As expected, all solutions lie in $`\mathscr{C}=\Pi`$, but the specific
projection depends on the generator $`F`$.

Non-separable Bregman generator. Here, we consider the generator

|     |                                                               |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       F(p)=\sum_{x=1}^{3}\left(\frac{1}{2}p(x)^{2}\right)+p(1)p(3).
       ```                                                            |     |

This generator is not separable, so we must minimize
$`G(p)=\mathsf{B}_{F}(p\parallel p_{0})`$ subject to $`p(1)=p(2)=q`$ and
$`p\in[0,1]^{3}`$. This is equivalent to minimizing
$`G(q,p_{3})=q^{2}+\frac{1}{2}p_{3}^{2}+qp_{3}-1.3q-0.5p_{3}`$ (see text
for derivation). The Hessian is

|     |                                       |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \nabla^{2}F(p)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&1\\
       0&1&0\\
       1&0&1\end{pmatrix},
       ```                                    |     |

which is positive semi-definite (eigenvalues $`0,1,2`$). The
unconstrained minimizer is $`(0.8,0.8,-0.3)`$, which is outside the
domain. A search of the boundaries of the feasible set $`[0,1]^{2}`$ for
$`(q,p_{3})`$ finds the minimum at $`(q,p_{3})=(0.65,0)`$. The
projection is thus

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{Non-Separable-Gen}}=(0.65,0.65,0)\in\mathscr{C},
``` |  |

which is distinct from previous projections.

Testing strictness non-trivially, a failure case. The strictness
assumption
($`\inf_{\pi\in\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}}\Psi_{F}(\pi)\geq\gamma>0`$)
is a strong condition. A meaningful test requires a scenario where the
competitor set $`\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ is non-empty. We can
construct a simple and sound case where this assumption fails.

Let the feasible set be the unconstrained cube,
$`\Pi=\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}=[0,1]^{3}`$. Let the generator be the
squared-Euclidean generator, $`F_{0}=F_{\mathrm{sq}}`$, so
$`\nabla F_{0}(p)=p`$. Now, to induce a non-trivial coherence set, we
choose a baseline $`\pi_{0}`$ that already has ties:
$`\pi_{0}=(0.5,0.5,0.2)`$. Since $`\pi_{0}`$ is already inside the
unconstrained set $`\Pi`$, the Bregman projection is just $`\pi_{0}`$
itself: $`\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}}=\pi_{0}=(0.5,0.5,0.2)`$.

The set $`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ is defined by the level-set partition of
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}}`$. Since
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}}(1)=\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}}(2)`$, the induced
partition is $`\mathscr{X}_{1}=\{1,2\},\mathscr{X}_{2}=\{3\}`$, and the
coherence set is:

|     |                                                                  |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}=\left\{\pi\in\Pi\colon\pi(1)=\pi(2)\right\}.
       ```                                                               |     |

In this case, $`\Pi`$ is the full cube, while $`\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ is
just the 2D slice of the cube where the first two coordinates are equal.
Therefore, the competitor set $`\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}`$ is
non-empty, and our test is non-trivial. We now check the infimum of the
functional $`\Psi_{F_{0}}(\pi)`$ over the competitor set:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Psi_{F_{0}}(\pi)=\E\left[\left\langle\nabla F_{0}(\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}})-\nabla F_{0}(\pi_{0}),\ \pi-\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}}\right\rangle\right].
``` |  |

Since $`\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}}=\pi_{0}`$, the gradient term is zero:
$`\nabla F_{0}(\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}})-\nabla F_{0}(\pi_{0})=\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}}-\pi_{0}=0`$.
Therefore, the functional is identically zero for all $`\pi`$:
$`\Psi_{F_{0}}(\pi)=\E\left[\left\langle 0,\ \pi-\widehat{\pi}_{F_{0}}\right\rangle\right]=0`$.
The strictness condition requires
$`\inf_{\pi\in\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}}\Psi_{F_{0}}(\pi)\geq\gamma>0`$.
However, our calculation shows:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\inf_{\pi\in\Pi\setminus\mathscr{C}_{F_{0}}}\Psi_{F_{0}}(\pi)=\inf_{\pi(1)\neq\pi(2)}0=0.
``` |  |

The strictness condition would require this infimum to be strictly
positive. Thus it fails, even though the competitor set is non-empty.
This is a “zero-slope” failure mode illustrating that strictness is a
genuinely non-trivial requirement, not implied by coherence or
feasibility alone.

#### 6.2.2 Interpretation and significance of rigidity

The rigidity theorems establish a surprising result: while the
mechanism’s outputs $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ vary with the choice of
generator $`F`$, their underlying level-set structure remains constant.
In this section, we interpret this universal coherence property and
discuss why it is a powerful and non-trivial consequence of the
stability assumption.

Interpretation of the Rigidity Result. Our rigidity theorems reveal a
powerful structural property of Bregman projections under a stability
assumption. For any given generator $`F`$, its output
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ induces a partition of the domain $`\mathscr{X}`$
based on its level sets. This partition, in turn, defines a *coherence
set* $`\mathscr{C}_{F}`$, an affine subspace of functions in $`\Pi`$
that are constant on those same level sets. The crucial conclusion of
the theorems is that this partition is, in fact, *universal*: it is
independent of the specific generator chosen. All generators in a
connected family are forced to produce outputs that are
piecewise-constant on the *exact same* underlying partition, defining a
single, universal coherence set $`\mathscr{C}`$.

Nontriviality of the Result. This result is far from trivial. A priori,
there is no reason to expect the level-set partitions to agree across
different generators. Each generator $`F`$ endows the space with a
different geometry, leading to a different Bregman divergence and, in
general, a different projection $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$. As we saw in our
numerical examples, the *values* of the projections
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ and $`\widehat{\pi}_{G}`$ can differ significantly
for generators $`F\neq G`$. The theorem’s surprising conclusion is that
despite these differences in values, the underlying *topological
structure* (the level-set partition) of all solutions must collapse to a
single, fundamental one. The stability assumption can be viewed as
acting as a powerful regularizer, forcing this structure to be rigid and
independent of the specific geometric "metric" ($`F`$) used for the
projection.

### 6.3 Rigidity under all divergences – Algebraic proof

An alternative proof that does not require the strictness assumption
imposes instead a structural assumption on $`\Pi`$. Here, we present a
proof in the case where $`\Pi`$ is a closed affine subspace. The
argument first establishes the result for the subclass of twice
continuously differentiable Legendre functions and then extends it to
all Legendre functions by invoking their density within the entire
family
(Lemma [31](#Thmtheorem31 "Lemma 31 (Local 𝐶^1-approximation by quadratic generators). ‣ B.1 Approximation by quadratic generators ‣ Appendix B Proofs for Characterization Theorems ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")).

###### 29Rigidity of Improvement for Affine Constraints.

Let $`\mathcal{F}`$ be the family of all Legendre functions. Let
$`\Pi\subset\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$ be a closed convex set that is also an
affine subspace, and let $`\pi_{0}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$. Suppose a
mechanism $`\mathcal{M}`$ produces for any $`F\in\mathcal{F}`$ an output
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\Pi`$ satisfying the strong improvement
inequality: for all $`\pi^{*}\in\Pi`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x))\right]\leq\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right].
``` |  |

Assume that the map $`F\mapsto\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is continuous from the
$`C^{1}`$ generator topology to $`L^{1}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}})`$
(stability). Then, there exists a measurable partition
$`\left\{\mathscr{X}_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}`$ of $`\mathscr{X}`$ such
that, defining the universal coherence set

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathscr{C}=\left\{\pi\in\Pi\colon\pi(x)=\pi(x^{\prime})\ \text{for a.e.\ }x,x^{\prime}\in\mathscr{X}_{i},\ \forall i\in I\right\},
``` |  |

we have $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\mathscr{C}`$ for all $`F\in\mathcal{F}`$.

###### Proof.

The hypothesis holds for all $`F\in\mathcal{F}`$ and thus the subset
$`\mathcal{F}_{C^{2}}`$. By Theorem
[30](#Thmtheorem30 "30Rigidity of Improvement for Affine Constraints – 𝐶^2 functions. ‣ 6.3 Rigidity under all divergences – Algebraic proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
this proves the existence of a universal partition
$`\left\{\mathscr{X}_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}`$ and a universal coherence
set $`\mathscr{C}`$ such that $`\widehat{\pi}_{G}\in\mathscr{C}`$ for
all $`G\in\mathcal{F}_{C^{2}}`$.

Now, select any function
$`F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}\setminus\mathcal{F}_{C^{2}}`$. The set
$`\mathcal{F}_{C^{2}}`$ is dense in $`\mathcal{F}`$ in the required
generator topology (see for example ([Rockafellar, 1997](#bib.bib10))).
Therefore, there exists a sequence of smooth generators
$`\{G_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{F}_{C^{2}}`$ such that
$`G_{n}\to F^{\prime}`$. By the stability assumption, this generator
convergence implies the convergence of their outputs in $`L^{1}`$:
$`\widehat{\pi}_{G_{n}}\to\widehat{\pi}_{F^{\prime}}`$ in
$`L^{1}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}})`$. Since $`G_{n}`$ is in
$`\mathcal{F}_{C^{2}}`$, $`\{\widehat{\pi}_{G_{n}}\}`$ belongs to the
set $`\mathscr{C}`$. The set $`\mathscr{C}`$ is defined by linear
equality constraints, making it a closed set in the $`L^{1}`$ topology.
Thus, the limit point $`\widehat{\pi}_{F^{\prime}}`$ must also be in
$`\mathscr{C}`$. Since this holds for any arbitrary $`F^{\prime}`$, we
have proven that $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\mathscr{C}`$ for all
$`F\in\mathcal{F}`$. ∎

We now prove the result for the special case of $`C^{2}`$ Legendre
functions.

###### 30Rigidity of Improvement for Affine Constraints – $`C^{2}`$ functions.

Let $`\mathcal{F}_{C^{2}}`$ be the family of all $`C^{2}`$ Legendre
generators. Let $`\Pi\subset\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$ be a closed convex set
that is also an affine subspace, and let
$`\pi_{0}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}`$. Suppose a mechanism $`\mathcal{M}`$
produces for any $`F\in\mathcal{F}_{C^{2}}`$ an output
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\Pi`$ satisfying the strong improvement
inequality: for all $`\pi^{*}\in\Pi`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x))\right]\leq\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\parallel\pi_{0}(x))\right].
``` |  |

Assume that the map $`F\mapsto\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is continuous from the
$`C^{1}`$ generator topology to $`L^{1}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}})`$
(stability). Then, there exists a measurable partition
$`\{\mathscr{X}_{i}\}_{i}`$ of $`\mathscr{X}`$ such that, defining the
universal coherence set

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathscr{C}:=\left\{\pi\in\Pi\colon\pi(x)=\pi(x^{\prime})\ \text{for a.e.\ }x,x^{\prime}\in\mathscr{X}_{i},\ \forall i\in I\right\},
``` |  |

we have $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\mathscr{C}`$ for every
$`F\in\mathcal{F}_{C^{2}}`$.

Before proceeding with the proof, we note that the strong improvement
inequality is exactly equivalent to the variational inequality (VI) used
in the proof. This equivalence is a direct consequence of the Bregman
three-point identity: For any $`\pi^{*},\widehat{\pi}_{F},\pi_{0}`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}\parallel\widehat{\pi}_{F})\right]=\left(\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi^{*}\parallel\pi_{0})\right]-\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\widehat{\pi}_{F}\parallel\pi_{0})\right]\right)-\E\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{F})-\nabla F(\pi_{0}),\pi^{*}-\widehat{\pi}_{F}\right\rangle\right].
``` |  |

The theorem’s hypothesis (the strong improvement inequality) is the
statement that the first term on the right-hand side is an upper bound
on the left-hand side. Thus, the theorem’s hypothesis is precisely
equivalent to the variational inequality:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E_{x\sim\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}}\left[\left\langle\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x))-\nabla F(\pi_{0}(x)),\pi^{*}(x)-\widehat{\pi}_{F}(x)\right\rangle\right]\geq 0,\qquad\forall\pi^{*}\in\Pi.
``` |  |

The proof that follows will rely directly on this variational form.

###### Proof.

For any $`F,G\in\mathcal{F}_{C^{2}}`$, we let
$`V_{F}=\nabla F(\widehat{\pi}_{F})-\nabla F(\pi_{0})`$ and write
$`A(F,G)=\E\left[\left\langle V_{F}-V_{G},\widehat{\pi}_{G}-\widehat{\pi}_{F}\right\rangle\right]`$.
A general four-point inequality, $`A(F,G)\geq 0`$, can be shown
straightforwardly to hold for all $`F,G\in\mathcal{F}_{C^{2}}`$, using
the variational inequalities.

The proof is in two main parts. First, we leverage the affine subspace
hypothesis to prove that the four-point inequality is always an equality
($`A(F,G)=0`$) in our context. Second, we use this fact to run a proof
by contradiction.

1. Four-Point Equilibrium, $`A(F,G)=0`$. Let $`\Pi=v_{0}+\Pi_{0}`$ be
our affine subspace, where $`\Pi_{0}`$ is the corresponding parallel
linear subspace.

The variation inequality (VI),
$`\E[\left\langle V_{F},\pi^{*}-\widehat{\pi}_{F}\right\rangle]\geq 0`$
holds for all $`\pi^{*}\in\Pi`$. Since $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\Pi`$, for
any vector $`v\in\Pi_{0}`$, the competitors
$`\pi^{*}=\widehat{\pi}_{F}+v`$ and $`\pi^{**}=\widehat{\pi}_{F}-v`$
both also lie in $`\Pi`$. Testing the inequality with both competitors
yields:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\E\left[\left\langle V_{F},\ (\widehat{\pi}_{F}+v)-\widehat{\pi}_{F}\right\rangle\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\E\left[\left\langle V_{F},\ v\right\rangle\right]\geq 0`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\E\left[\left\langle V_{F},\ (\widehat{\pi}_{F}-v)-\widehat{\pi}_{F}\right\rangle\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\E\left[\left\langle V_{F},\ -v\right\rangle\right]\geq 0.`$ |  |

These two facts force the variation inequality to be an equality. The
error vector $`V_{F}`$ must be orthogonal to the subspace $`\Pi_{0}`$:
$`\E\left[\left\langle V_{F},\ v\right\rangle\right]=0`$, for all
$`v\in\Pi_{0}`$. This holds for any generator. Thus, for any pair
$`(F,G)`$, we have $`\E[\langle V_{F},v\rangle]=0`$ and
$`\E[\langle V_{G},v\rangle]=0`$.

By hypothesis, $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}\in\Pi`$ and
$`\widehat{\pi}_{G}\in\Pi`$. Their difference
$`\Delta_{F,G}=\widehat{\pi}_{G}-\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ must therefore lie
in the parallel subspace $`\Pi_{0}`$. Since $`\Delta_{F,G}\in\Pi_{0}`$,
we can use it as the test vector $`v`$ in both orthogonality conditions,
which gives:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E[\langle V_{F},\Delta_{F,G}\rangle]=0\quad\text{and}\quad\E[\langle V_{G},\Delta_{F,G}\rangle]=0.
``` |  |

The four-point term is the difference of these two zeros:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A(F,G)=\E[\langle V_{F}-V_{G},\Delta_{F,G}\rangle]=\E[\langle V_{F},\Delta_{F,G}\rangle]-\E[\langle V_{G},\Delta_{F,G}\rangle]=0-0=0.
``` |  |

This proves Part 1.

##### 2. Contradiction via Perturbation.

Assume by contradiction that there exists a non-rigid pair
$`(F_{1},F_{2})`$ with
$`\Delta=\widehat{\pi}_{2}-\widehat{\pi}_{1}\not\equiv 0`$. By the
Lusin-based approximation
(Lemma [33](#Thmtheorem33 "Lemma 33 (Localized approximation of Hessians). ‣ B.3 Localized approximation of Hessians ‣ Appendix B Proofs for Characterization Theorems ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")),
for any $`\eta>0`$, we can find a compact set $`K\subset\mathscr{X}`$
with $`\mu(\mathscr{X}\setminus K)<\eta`$ and a finite measurable
partition $`\{K_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}`$ of $`K`$ such that on each $`K_{j}`$,
the relevant functions are $`\eta`$-close to a set of constant
representatives:

- •
  $`\sup_{x\in K_{j}}\left\|\widehat{\pi}_{k}(x)-q_{k,j}\right\|\leq\eta`$
  (for $`k=0,1,2`$, where $`\widehat{\pi}_{0}=\pi_{0}`$);
- •
  $`\sup_{x\in K_{j}}\left\|\nabla^{2}F_{k}(x)-M_{k,j}\right\|\leq\eta`$
  (for $`k=1,2`$).

Let $`w_{k,j}=q_{k,j}-q_{0,j}`$ and $`\Delta_{j}=q_{2,j}-q_{1,j}`$.
Since $`\Delta\not\equiv 0`$, we can choose the partition fine enough to
ensure that for at least one $`j`$, $`\mu(K_{j})>0`$ and
$`\Delta_{j}\neq 0`$, which guarantees that
$`-\epsilon_{0}=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu(K_{j})\left\|\Delta_{j}\right\|^{2}<0`$.
This data defines a continuous, linear functional $`\Psi`$ which maps
lists of Hessian matrices to a scalar. For two lists
$`\mathscr{S}_{1}=\{S_{1,j}\}_{j=1}^{m}`$ and
$`\mathscr{S}_{2}=\{S_{2,j}\}_{j=1}^{m}`$, we define:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Psi(\mathscr{S}_{1},\mathscr{S}_{2})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu(K_{j})\left\langle S_{1,j}w_{1,j}-S_{2,j}w_{2,j},\Delta_{j}\right\rangle.
``` |  |

Let $`\mathcal{I}=\{I\}_{j=1}^{m}`$ be the list of identity matrices.
The functional $`\Psi`$ evaluated at $`(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})`$ is:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Psi(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu(K_{j})\left\langle Iw_{1,j}-Iw_{2,j},\Delta_{j}\right\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu(K_{j})\left\langle-\Delta_{j},\Delta_{j}\right\rangle=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu(K_{j})\|\Delta_{j}\|^{2}=-\epsilon_{0}<0.
``` |  |

Let $`\mathcal{M}_{1}=\{M_{1,j}\}_{j=1}^{m}`$ and
$`\mathcal{M}_{2}=\{M_{2,j}\}_{j=1}^{m}`$ be the lists of representative
Hessians for $`F_{1}`$ and $`F_{2}`$, respectively. By the local
$`C^{1}`$-approximation
(Lemma [31](#Thmtheorem31 "Lemma 31 (Local 𝐶^1-approximation by quadratic generators). ‣ B.1 Approximation by quadratic generators ‣ Appendix B Proofs for Characterization Theorems ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")),
the true integral $`A(F_{1},F_{2})`$ is approximated by the functional
$`\Psi`$ evaluated at these constant Hessians. By choosing the Lusin
parameter $`\eta`$ sufficiently small, we can make the approximation
error, $`\delta_{A}`$, arbitrarily small.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\delta_{A}(\eta)=|\Psi(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2})|=|A(F_{1},F_{2})-\Psi(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2})|,\quad\text{where }\delta_{A}(\eta)\to 0\text{ as }\eta\to 0.
``` |  |

Fix a small perturbation scale $`\alpha>0`$. We choose $`\alpha`$ small
enough to satisfy the Legendre constraints of the bump lemma. Construct
the generators $`G_{k}=F_{k}+\sum_{j}h_{kj}`$, where the bumps
$`h_{kj}`$ are chosen (via
Lemma [32](#Thmtheorem32 "Lemma 32 (Localized Hessian Perturbation). ‣ B.2 Localized Hessian perturbation ‣ Appendix B Proofs for Characterization Theorems ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"))
to satisfy:

1.  1.
    $`\nabla^{2}h_{kj}(q_{kj})=\alpha(I-M_{kj})`$.
2.  2.
    The global $`C^{1}`$-norm of the perturbation is small:
    $`\|G_{k}-F_{k}\|_{C^{1}}<\delta_{bump}`$.

This yields new generators $`G_{k}`$ that are Legendre (since $`\alpha`$
is small) and $`C^{1}`$-close to $`F_{k}`$. The local Hessians of
$`G_{k}`$ are the target list
$`\mathscr{S}_{G,k}=\{(1-\alpha)M_{kj}+\alpha I\}`$.

By the stability assumption, choosing $`\delta_{bump}`$ small ensures
the outputs are close, which in turn means all approximations are
controlled. We can choose our parameters (Lusin $`\eta`$ and bump-norm
$`\delta_{bump}`$) small enough to make the total approximation error
$`\epsilon_{approx}`$ between the true integral and the
$`\Psi`$-functional arbitrarily small.

We know that the inequality $`A(G_{1},G_{2})\geq 0`$ holds for all
Legendre functions $`G_{1},G_{2}`$. By the stability assumption and the
Lusin lemmas (Lemmas
[31](#Thmtheorem31 "Lemma 31 (Local 𝐶^1-approximation by quadratic generators). ‣ B.1 Approximation by quadratic generators ‣ Appendix B Proofs for Characterization Theorems ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")
and
[33](#Thmtheorem33 "Lemma 33 (Localized approximation of Hessians). ‣ B.3 Localized approximation of Hessians ‣ Appendix B Proofs for Characterization Theorems ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")),
the true integral $`A(G_{1},G_{2})`$ is a continuous function of its
underlying components. We can therefore choose our parameters (Lusin
$`\eta`$ and bump-norm $`\delta_{bump}`$) small enough to make the total
approximation error $`\epsilon_{approx}`$ between the true integral and
its piecewise-constant model,
$`\Psi(\mathscr{S}_{G,1},\mathscr{S}_{G,2})`$, arbitrarily small:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\left\lvert A(G_{1},G_{2})-\Psi(\mathscr{S}_{G,1},\mathscr{S}_{G,2})\right\rvert\leq\epsilon_{approx}.
``` |  |

Combining this error bound with the fact that $`A(G_{1},G_{2})\geq 0`$
yields the key inequality for our contradiction:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Psi(\mathscr{S}_{G,1},\mathscr{S}_{G,2})\geq A(G_{1},G_{2})-\epsilon_{approx}\geq-\epsilon_{approx}.
``` |  |

We can calculate the value of
$`\Psi(\mathscr{S}_{G,1},\mathscr{S}_{G,2})`$ explicitly:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Psi(\mathscr{S}_{G,1},\mathscr{S}_{G,2})`$ | $`\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)\Psi(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2})+\alpha\Psi(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)\Psi(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2})-\alpha\epsilon_{0}.`$ |  |

This gives us the inequality:
$`(1-\alpha)\Psi(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2})-\alpha\epsilon_{0}\geq-\epsilon_{approx}`$.
We know $`|\Psi(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2})|\leq\delta_{A}`$. Since
$`(1-\alpha)>0`$, this means
$`(1-\alpha)\Psi(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2})\geq-(1-\alpha)\delta_{A}`$.
We use the upper bound:
$`\Psi(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2})\leq\delta_{A}`$. This gives:
$`\alpha\epsilon_{0}\leq(1-\alpha)\Psi(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2})+\epsilon_{approx}\leq(1-\alpha)\delta_{A}+\epsilon_{approx}`$,
and the final inequality
$`\alpha\epsilon_{0}\leq(1-\alpha)\delta_{A}+\epsilon_{approx}`$.

This is a contradiction: The term on the left, $`\alpha\epsilon_{0}`$,
is a fixed positive number (since we fixed a small $`\alpha>0`$ and
$`\epsilon_{0}>0`$); the term on the right,
$`(1-\alpha)\delta_{A}+\epsilon_{approx}`$, is the sum of our
approximation errors, which we can make arbitrarily small by choosing
$`\eta`$ and $`\delta_{bump}`$ to be small enough. We can choose our
parameters such that the total error is strictly less that
$`\alpha\epsilon_{0}/2`$, which leads to the false statement
$`\alpha\epsilon_{0}\leq\alpha\epsilon_{0}/2`$. This contradiction
proves that the initial assumption (a non-rigid pair
$`\Delta\not\equiv 0`$) must be false. This completes the proof. ∎

These results are already very compelling, establishing the rigidity
property across two major, distinct classes of problems. The algebraic
proof
(Theorem [30](#Thmtheorem30 "30Rigidity of Improvement for Affine Constraints – 𝐶^2 functions. ‣ 6.3 Rigidity under all divergences – Algebraic proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"))
demonstrates that rigidity holds for the entire class of affine
subspaces by leveraging their unique orthogonality properties (i.e.,
$`A(F,G)=0`$). In parallel, the topological proof
(Theorem [28](#Thmtheorem28 "28Topological rigidity. ‣ 6.2 Rigidity under all divergences – Topological proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"))
provides a robust framework for a different, broad class of sets, such
as polyhedral sets, by replacing the strong geometric assumption with
the analytical strictness condition.

While this does not yet cover all possible convex sets, such as smoothly
curved $`L^{2}`$-balls, where both of these current proof strategies can
fail, these theorems lay a clear foundation. It is likely that a more
general proof could be developed by building on these ideas. A promising
direction for future work, for example, is to find a weaker condition
than strictness that is still sufficient to guarantee the stickiness of
the solution path in the topological proof, which would extend the
result to a more general class of curved sets.

#### 6.3.1 Toy examples

In this section, we present and discuss two simple examples illustrating
Theorem [29](#Thmtheorem29 "29Rigidity of Improvement for Affine Constraints. ‣ 6.3 Rigidity under all divergences – Algebraic proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement").

Example with affine constraints Here, we give a concrete nontrivial
example where $`\Pi`$ is affine, a hyperplane, and where
Theorem [29](#Thmtheorem29 "29Rigidity of Improvement for Affine Constraints. ‣ 6.3 Rigidity under all divergences – Algebraic proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")
applies, without any separate strictness hypothesis. The induced
coherence set $`\mathscr{C}`$ (functions constant on the block
$`\left\{1,2\right\}`$) is nontrivial and arises from the interaction of
the symmetry of $`\Pi`$ with the symmetry of the baseline $`\pi_{0}`$,
not from an explicit equality constraint in $`\Pi`$.

Let $`\mathscr{X}=\left\{1,2,3\right\}`$ and
$`\mathscr{Y}=\left\{0,1\right\}`$. Identify a conditional model $`\pi`$
with the vector $`p=(p(1),p(2),p(3))`$ where $`p(x)=\Pr(y=1\mid x)`$.
Take $`\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}`$ to be the uniform distribution on
$`\mathscr{X}`$. Define the affine feasible set $`\Pi`$ by the single
linear constraint

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Pi=\left\{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}\colon p(1)+p(2)+p(3)=S\right\},
``` |  |

for some fixed constant $`S\in(0,3)`$ (so $`\Pi`$ is an affine
hyperplane in $`\mathbb{R}^{3}`$ intersected with the cube
$`[0,1]^{3}`$). Note that $`\Pi`$ is an affine set and, importantly for
our example, it is invariant under the swap (permutation) of coordinates
$`1\leftrightarrow 2`$.

Choose a baseline $`\pi_{0}`$ that is symmetric in coordinates $`1`$ and
$`2`$, but does not lie in $`\Pi`$. For instance take

|     |                                                        |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \pi_{0}=(a,a,b),\qquad a,b\in(0,1),\qquad a+a+b\neq S.
       ```                                                     |     |

Concretely, one may pick $`a=0.30,\ b=0.60`$ and $`S=1.0`$, so that
$`\pi_{0}=(0.30,0.30,0.60)`$ with sum $`1.20\neq 1.0`$.

Fix any Legendre generator $`F`$ (e.g. KL or squared Euclidean).
Consider the Bregman projection of $`\pi_{0}`$ onto the affine set
$`\Pi`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\widehat{\pi}_{F}=\argmin_{\pi\in\Pi}\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi(x)\|\pi_{0}(x))\right].
``` |  |

Since $`\Pi`$ is invariant under the transposition $`\tau`$ that swaps
indices $`1`$ and $`2`$, and since the baseline $`\pi_{0}`$ satisfies
$`\tau\pi_{0}=\pi_{0}`$ (that is, $`\pi_{0}`$ is symmetric on the pair
$`\left\{1,2\right\}`$), the following standard symmetry argument shows
the projection must be symmetric as well.

Let $`J(\pi)=\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\pi\|\pi_{0})\right]`$ denote the
strictly convex objective. For any feasible $`\pi\in\Pi`$ the swapped
vector $`\tau\pi`$ is also feasible (since $`\Pi`$ is swap-invariant)
and satisfies
$`J(\tau\pi)=\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\tau\pi\|\tau\pi_{0})\right]=\E\left[\mathsf{B}_{F}(\tau\pi\|\pi_{0})\right]`$,
because $`\tau\pi_{0}=\pi_{0}`$. Therefore $`J(\pi)`$ and $`J(\tau\pi)`$
are equal for every feasible $`\pi`$. By uniqueness of the minimizer
(strict convexity), the minimizer must be fixed by the swap:
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}=\tau\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$.

Thus $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}(1)=\widehat{\pi}_{F}(2)`$ and the projection
$`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is block-constant on $`\left\{1,2\right\}`$ for
*every* Legendre generator $`F`$. The induced partition is therefore
nontrivial (it merges inputs 1 and 2 into the same block:
$`\mathscr{X}_{1}=\left\{1,2\right\}`$, while
$`\mathscr{X}_{2}=\left\{3\right\}`$) even though the affine constraint
defining $`\Pi`$ did not itself force $`p(1)=p(2)`$.

The conclusion that $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ is block-constant on
$`\left\{1,2\right\}`$ for all Legendre $`F`$ is immediate from the
symmetry and uniqueness argument and illustrates the rigidity phenomenon
in a nontrivial setting.

Example with an asymmetric baseline This example illustrates
Theorem [29](#Thmtheorem29 "29Rigidity of Improvement for Affine Constraints. ‣ 6.3 Rigidity under all divergences – Algebraic proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")
using a different construction. Here, the affine set $`\Pi`$ itself
enforces the block-constant structure, and this structure is imposed on
an asymmetric baseline $`\pi_{0}`$.

Let $`\mathscr{X}=\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}`$ with
$`\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}`$ a uniform distribution. Define $`\Pi`$ as
an affine subspace that forces two separate blocks to be constant:
$`\Pi=\left\{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}\colon p(1)=p(2)\text{ and }p(3)=p(4)\right\}`$.
We choose a baseline $`\pi_{0}`$ that is not symmetric and does not lie
in $`\Pi`$: $`\pi_{0}=(0.1,0.3,0.9,0.5)`$. The mechanism $`\mathcal{M}`$
is the Bregman projection $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}=\Pi_{\Pi}^{F}(\pi_{0})`$.
This satisfies the theorem’s assumptions, as $`\Pi`$ is affine.

Since the constraints on blocks $`\left\{1,2\right\}`$ and
$`\left\{3,4\right\}`$ are independent, the projection problem
decouples.

A. Squared-Euclidean Generator ($`F_{\mathrm{sq}}`$) The projection
$`\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{sq}}`$ minimizes the $`L_{2}`$ distance. The
solution for each block is the mean of the $`\pi_{0}`$ values in that
block.

- •
  Block 1:
  $`q_{1}=p(1)=p(2)=\frac{p_{0}(1)+p_{0}(2)}{2}=\frac{0.1+0.3}{2}=0.20`$.
- •
  Block 2:
  $`q_{2}=p(3)=p(4)=\frac{p_{0}(3)+p_{0}(4)}{2}=\frac{0.9+0.5}{2}=0.70`$.

The projection is:
$`\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{sq}}=(0.20,0.20,0.70,0.70)`$.

B. Negative-Entropy / KL Generator ($`F_{\mathrm{KL}}`$) The projection
$`\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{KL}}`$ is the Bregman centroid, which for this
generator is the geometric mean of the $`\pi_{0}`$ values in each block.

- •
  Block 1:
  $`q_{1}=p(1)=p(2)=\sqrt{p_{0}(1)\times p_{0}(2)}=\sqrt{0.1\times 0.3}=\sqrt{0.03}\approx 0.173`$.
- •
  Block 2:
  $`q_{2}=p(3)=p(4)=\sqrt{p_{0}(3)\times p_{0}(4)}=\sqrt{0.9\times 0.5}=\sqrt{0.45}\approx 0.671`$.

The projection is:
$`\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{KL}}\approx(0.173,0.173,0.671,0.671)`$.

As the theorem predicted, the rigidity phenomenon is observed. The
values of the projections are different, but
$`\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{sq}}`$ and $`\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{KL}}`$
induce the same partition: $`\mathscr{X}_{1}=\left\{1,2\right\}`$,
````
