````math
$`\mathscr{X}_{2}=\left\{3,4\right\}`$. The structure of the output is
rigid and identical for all generators $`F`$ because it is dictated
entirely by the affine subspace $`\Pi`$.

#### 6.3.2 Extension via kernel methods

The most direct way to extend the algebraic proof beyond affine sets,
using a simple and powerful idea, is indeed *kernel methods*.

The algebraic proof
(Theorem [29](#Thmtheorem29 "29Rigidity of Improvement for Affine Constraints. ‣ 6.3 Rigidity under all divergences – Algebraic proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"))
based on the affine nature of $`\Pi`$ provides a parallel linear
subspace $`\Pi_{0}`$. This is the key that allows us to test the
variational inequality with both $`+v`$ and $`-v`$ for any
$`v\in\Pi_{0}`$, which in turn proves the crucial "orthogonality"
condition:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\E\left[\left\langle V_{F},\ v\right\rangle\right]=0,\quad\forall v\in\Pi_{0}.
``` |  |

This orthogonality is what forces the four-point term $`A(F,G)`$ to be
zero, completing the proof. The extension idea is to apply this same
method in a high-dimensional feature space where $`\Pi`$ becomes affine:

- •
  Mapping to a feature space: We can use a positive definite
  kernel-based feature mapping
  $`\phi\colon\Pi_{\mathrm{all}}\to\mathbb{H}`$ to map our models
  $`\pi`$ from their original space to a high-dimensional Reproducing
  Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS), $`\mathbb{H}`$.
- •
  Linearizing the set: If we can find a kernel such that the image of
  our (non-affine) set $`\Pi`$, denoted $`\phi(\Pi)`$, is an affine
  subspace in $`\mathbb{H}`$, then the entire algebraic proof holds.
- •
  Applying the proof in $`\mathbb{H}`$: The variational inequality would
  be expressed using the inner product in $`\mathbb{H}`$:

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  \left\langle V_{F},\phi(\pi^{*})-\phi(\widehat{\pi}_{F})\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\geq 0,\qquad\forall\pi^{*}\in\Pi.
  ``` |  |

  Here, $`V_{F}`$ is the error vector in the dual of $`\mathbb{H}`$.
  Since $`\phi(\Pi)`$ is an affine subspace, the exact same
  orthogonality argument applies: $`V_{F}`$ must be orthogonal to the
  parallel linear subspace $`\phi(\Pi)_{0}`$. This again forces
  $`A(F,G)=0`$, where $`A`$ is now defined using the
  $`\mathbb{H}`$-inner product.

This *kernelization* would prove that the mapped outputs,
$`\phi(\widehat{\pi}_{F})`$, must have a universal block-constant
structure in the feature space $`\mathbb{H}`$. This implies that the
original outputs $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ must also be rigid, conforming to
a potentially non-linear structure defined by the pre-image of that
universal partition in $`\mathbb{H}`$.

#### 6.3.3 Example: linearizing a spherical constraint via kernel methods

Here, we illustrate the kernel approach with a very simple non-affine
feasible set that becomes affine after a nonlinear feature mapping. Let
$`\mathscr{X}=\left\{1,2\right\}`$ and
$`\pi=(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}`$. Consider the (non-affine)
quadratic constraint:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Pi=\left\{\pi\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}:p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}=1\right\},
``` |  |

the positive quadrant of the unit circle. Since $`\Pi`$ is curved, the
algebraic proof of
Theorem [29](#Thmtheorem29 "29Rigidity of Improvement for Affine Constraints. ‣ 6.3 Rigidity under all divergences – Algebraic proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")
does not apply in the original space. We now use a polynomial kernel to
*linearize* this constraint. Define the nonlinear feature map

|     |                                                  |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \phi(\pi)=(p_{1}^{2},\,p_{2}^{2})=(z_{1},z_{2}).
       ```                                               |     |

For any $`\pi\in\Pi`$, we have $`z_{1}+z_{2}=1`$, so

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\phi(\Pi)=\left\{z\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}:z_{1}+z_{2}=1\right\},
``` |  |

which is a line segment, an affine subset of the feature space
$`\mathbb{H}`$.

Thus, in the feature space, the feasibility region is affine, and the
projections $`\phi(\widehat{\pi}_{F})`$ satisfy the orthogonality
condition of
Theorem [29](#Thmtheorem29 "29Rigidity of Improvement for Affine Constraints. ‣ 6.3 Rigidity under all divergences – Algebraic proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement").
The rigidity argument therefore applies directly in $`\mathbb{H}`$.

We can further illustrate this with a numerical example. Take an
asymmetric baseline $`\pi_{0}=(1.0,0.5)`$ and consider two generating
functions.

A. Squared Euclidean generator ($`F_{\mathrm{sq}}`$):

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{sq}}=\argmin_{p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}=1}\frac{1}{2}\bigl((p_{1}-1.0)^{2}+(p_{2}-0.5)^{2}\bigr)\approx(0.894,0.447).
``` |  |

B. Weighted squared generator ($`F_{W}`$):.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
F_{W}(p)=\tfrac{1}{2}(p_{1}^{2}+10p_{2}^{2}),\qquad\widehat{\pi}_{W}\approx(0.985,0.174).
``` |  |

Their images under $`\phi`$ both lie on the affine set $`\phi(\Pi)`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\phi(\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{sq}})\approx(0.80,0.20),\qquad\phi(\widehat{\pi}_{W})\approx(0.97,0.03).
``` |  |

| Generator ($`F`$) | $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ in original space | $`\phi(\widehat{\pi}_{F})`$ in feature space |
|----|----|----|
| $`F_{\mathrm{sq}}`$ (L2) | $`(0.894,0.447)`$ | $`(0.80,0.20)`$ |
| $`F_{W}`$ (Weighted L2) | $`(0.985,0.174)`$ | $`(0.97,0.03)`$ |

Table 1: Projections in the original and feature spaces.

Although the two Bregman projections differ visibly on the curved set
$`\Pi`$, their mapped projections lie on the same affine segment
$`\phi(\Pi)`$.
Theorem [29](#Thmtheorem29 "29Rigidity of Improvement for Affine Constraints. ‣ 6.3 Rigidity under all divergences – Algebraic proof ‣ 6 Characterization of Improvement Mechanisms ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")
therefore forces the mapped outputs to share a universal level-set
structure. This yields a corresponding nonlinear rigidity for the
original projections $`\widehat{\pi}_{F}`$ on $`\Pi`$.

## 7 Conclusion

We addressed the fundamental challenge of designing reliably
self-improving systems. While existing approaches are often effective in
practice, they largely rely on heuristics and lack formal guarantees. To
overcome this, we introduced a principled framework for self-improvement
based on the concept of *coherence*, which enforces consistency of model
outputs under task-preserving input transformations. Our theoretical
analysis shows that iteratively increasing coherence leads to monotonic
improvement, formalized through guaranteed reductions in expected
Bregman divergence.

A key contribution of this work is our *characterization theorem*, which
establishes that any mechanism providing robust improvement guarantees
must align with a coherence-based structure, under some broad
assumptions. This result elevates our framework from a specific method
to a foundational principle, offering a rigorous lens for designing and
analyzing self-improving systems.

We leave it to future work to further validate these methods on diverse
benchmarks, explore relaxed assumptions, extend to multi-agent settings,
and further investigate multiple connections to information geometry and
causal inference. By providing a solid theoretical foundation, we hope
to enable the development of self-improving models that are not only
more capable but also predictable, reliable, and safe.

## Appendix A Proofs of Bregman Divergence Results

This section contains the detailed proofs of the lemmas and technical
results on Bregman divergences used throughout the paper.

### A.1 Centroid properties and projection lemmas

See
[3](#Thmtheorem3 "Lemma 3. ‣ 2.2.3 Centroid properties ‣ 2.2 Bregman divergences and Legendre functions ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")

###### Proof.

By definition of the Bregman divergence:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}_{k}\parallel\mathsf{q})=F(\mathsf{p}_{k})-F(\mathsf{q})-\langle\nabla F(\mathsf{q}),\mathsf{p}_{k}-\mathsf{q}\rangle.
``` |  |

Summing over $`k`$ with weights $`\lambda_{k}`$ gives

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}_{k}\parallel\mathsf{q})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}F(\mathsf{p}_{k})-F(\mathsf{q})-\langle\nabla F(\mathsf{q}),\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}(\mathsf{p}_{k}-\mathsf{q})\rangle.
``` |  |

Similarly,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{B}_{F}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\mathsf{p}_{k}\parallel\mathsf{q}\Big)=F\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\mathsf{p}_{k}\Big)-F(\mathsf{q})-\langle\nabla F(\mathsf{q}),\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\mathsf{p}_{k}-\mathsf{q}\rangle.
``` |  |

Subtracting the second from the first yields

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}F(\mathsf{p}_{k})-F\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\mathsf{p}_{k}\Big),
``` |  |

which is independent of $`\mathsf{q}`$, as claimed. ∎

See
[4](#Thmtheorem4 "Lemma 4. ‣ 2.2.3 Centroid properties ‣ 2.2 Bregman divergences and Legendre functions ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")

###### Proof.

By the duality identity for Legendre functions:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\mathsf{B}_{F}(\mathsf{p}\parallel\mathsf{q}_{k})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\mathsf{B}_{F^{*}}(\nabla F(\mathsf{q}_{k})\parallel\nabla F(\mathsf{p})).
``` |  |

Using
Lemma [3](#Thmtheorem3 "Lemma 3. ‣ 2.2.3 Centroid properties ‣ 2.2 Bregman divergences and Legendre functions ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"),
this is equivalent, up to an additive constant independent of
$`\mathsf{p}`$, to

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{B}_{F^{*}}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\nabla F(\mathsf{q}_{k})\parallel\nabla F(\mathsf{p})\Big)=\mathsf{B}_{F}\Big(\mathsf{p}\parallel(\nabla F)^{-1}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\nabla F(\mathsf{q}_{k})\Big)\Big).
``` |  |

Thus, the unconstrained minimizer is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{p}^{*}=(\nabla F)^{-1}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\nabla F(\mathsf{q}_{k})\Big).
``` |  |

For a closed convex set $`\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{K}`$, the
minimizer over $`K`$ is given by the Bregman projection of
$`\mathsf{p}^{*}`$ onto $`K`$, as required. ∎

See
[5](#Thmtheorem5 "Corollary 5. ‣ 2.2.3 Centroid properties ‣ 2.2 Bregman divergences and Legendre functions ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")

###### Proof.

Immediate from
Lemma [4](#Thmtheorem4 "Lemma 4. ‣ 2.2.3 Centroid properties ‣ 2.2 Bregman divergences and Legendre functions ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement"):
the minimum of the sum over $`K`$ equals the sum evaluated at the
unconstrained centroid plus the divergence between the projection
$`\mathsf{p}^{*}_{\mathscr{C}}`$ and the unconstrained centroid
$`\mathsf{p}^{*}`$. ∎

### A.2 Fenchel-Bregman inequality

See
[6](#Thmtheorem6 "Lemma 6. ‣ 2.2.4 Fenchel-Bregman inequality ‣ 2.2 Bregman divergences and Legendre functions ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Coherence Mechanisms for Provable Self-Improvement")

###### Proof.

By definition of the Bregman divergences:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{F}(u\parallel v)+\mathsf{B}_{F^{*}}(\alpha\parallel\beta)-\langle u-v,\alpha-\beta\rangle`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=F(u)-F(v)-\langle\nabla F(v),u-v\rangle+F^{*}(\alpha)-F^{*}(\beta)-\langle\nabla F^{*}(\beta),\alpha-\beta\rangle-\langle u-v,\alpha-\beta\rangle`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\big(F(u)+F^{*}(\alpha)-\langle u,\alpha\rangle\big)-\big(F(v)+F^{*}(\beta)-\langle v,\beta\rangle\big)+\langle u-v,\beta-\nabla F(v)\rangle+\langle\nabla F^{*}(\beta)-v,\alpha-\beta\rangle.`$ |  |

Now set $`\beta=\nabla F(v)`$. Since $`F`$ is Legendre,
$`\nabla F^{*}=(\nabla F)^{-1}`$, so $`\nabla F^{*}(\beta)=v`$. The last
two inner-product terms vanish. By Fenchel duality,
$`F(u)+F^{*}(\alpha)-\langle u,\alpha\rangle\geq 0`$ and
$`F(v)+F^{*}(\beta)-\langle v,\beta\rangle=0`$, completing the proof. ∎

## Appendix B Proofs for Characterization Theorems

### B.1 Approximation by quadratic generators

###### Lemma 31 (Local $`C^{1}`$-approximation by quadratic generators).

Let $`F`$ be a $`C^{2}`$ Legendre generator on an open convex domain
$`\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}`$. Let $`K\subset\Omega`$ be a compact
convex set. Fix $`q_{0}\in K`$ and let

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
Q_{0}(q)=F(q_{0})+\langle\nabla F(q_{0}),q-q_{0}\rangle+\tfrac{1}{2}(q-q_{0})^{\top}\nabla^{2}F(q_{0})(q-q_{0}).
``` |  |

Then $`Q_{0}`$ is a quadratic Legendre generator. Moreover, for every
$`\epsilon>0`$ there exists $`\delta>0`$ (depending on $`F`$, $`K`$,
$`q_{0}`$, and $`\epsilon`$) such that for all
$`q\in B(q_{0},\delta)\cap K`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
|F(q)-Q_{0}(q)|<\epsilon,\qquad\left\|\nabla F(q)-\nabla Q_{0}(q)\right\|<\epsilon.
``` |  |

###### Proof.

Since $`F\in C^{2}(\Omega)`$ and is Legendre,
$`S_{0}=\nabla^{2}F(q_{0})\succ 0`$, so $`Q_{0}`$ is a quadratic
Legendre generator. Since $`\nabla^{2}F`$ is continuous on the compact
$`K`$, it is uniformly continuous there. Let $`\omega(\cdot)`$ be a
modulus of continuity for $`\nabla^{2}F`$ on $`K`$: for all
$`c,c^{\prime}\in K`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\left\|\nabla^{2}F(c)-\nabla^{2}F(c^{\prime})\right\|\leq\omega(\left\|c-c^{\prime}\right\|),
``` |  |

with $`\omega(t)\to 0`$ as $`t\downarrow 0`$. Fix $`\epsilon>0`$. Since
$`\omega(t)\to 0`$ as $`t\downarrow 0`$, it follows that
$`\omega(t)t\to 0`$ and $`\omega(t)t^{2}\to 0`$. Thus, we can choose
$`\delta>0`$ so small that

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\omega(\delta)\delta\leq\epsilon\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tfrac{1}{2}\omega(\delta)\delta^{2}\leq\epsilon.
``` |  |

Such a $`\delta>0`$ exists because both $`\omega(t)t`$ and
$`\omega(t)t^{2}`$ tend to $`0`$ as $`t\to 0`$. For any
$`q\in B(q_{0},\delta)\cap K`$ define $`c_{t}=q_{0}+t(q-q_{0})\in K`$
for $`t\in[0,1]`$. By the integral form of the remainder,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\nabla F(q)-\nabla Q_{0}(q)=\int_{0}^{1}\left(\nabla^{2}F(c_{t})-\nabla^{2}F(q_{0})\right)(q-q_{0})\,dt.
``` |  |

Hence, as
$`\left\|c_{t}-q_{0}\right\|\leq\left\|q-q_{0}\right\|<\delta`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\|\nabla F(q)-\nabla Q_{0}(q)\|\leq\int_{0}^{1}\left\|\nabla^{2}F(c_{t})-\nabla^{2}F(q_{0})\right\|\|q-q_{0}\|\,dt\leq\omega(\delta)\,\|q-q_{0}\|<\omega(\delta)\,\delta\leq\epsilon,
``` |  |

using the first condition on $`\delta`$. For the function value, Taylor
expansion with integral remainder gives

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
F(q)-Q_{0}(q)=\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\,(q-q_{0})^{\top}\big(\nabla^{2}F(c_{t})-\nabla^{2}F(q_{0})\big)(q-q_{0})\,dt.
``` |  |

Thus, we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
|F(q)-Q_{0}(q)|\leq\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\left\|\nabla^{2}F(c_{t})-\nabla^{2}F(q_{0})\right\|\left\|q-q_{0}\right\|^{2}\,dt\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\omega(\delta)\,\|q-q_{0}\|^{2}<\tfrac{1}{2}\omega(\delta)\,\delta^{2}\leq\epsilon,
``` |  |

using the second condition on $`\delta`$. ∎

### B.2 Localized Hessian perturbation

###### Lemma 32 (Localized Hessian Perturbation).

Let $`K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ be compact. Let $`F`$ be $`C^{2}`$ on an
open neighborhood of $`K`$. Fix $`q\in K`$ and let $`H`$ be any
symmetric $`d\times d`$ matrix. Then for every $`\delta>0`$, every
$`\alpha>0`$, and every neighborhood $`U`$ of $`q`$, there exists a
smooth function $`h\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}`$ and a constant
$`C`$ (depending only on the choice of bump function) such that:

1.  1.
    $`\supp(h)\subset U`$.
2.  2.
    $`\sup_{\pi\in K}\left\|\nabla h(\pi)\right\|\leq\delta`$.
3.  3.
    $`\nabla^{2}h(q)=\alpha H`$.
4.  4.
    $`\sup_{\pi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\nabla^{2}h(\pi)\right\|\leq C\alpha\left\|H\right\|`$.

Moreover, if $`\nabla^{2}F(\pi)\succeq\lambda I`$ on $`K`$ for some
$`\lambda>0`$, then by choosing $`\alpha`$ sufficiently small (e.g.,
$`\alpha<\lambda/(C\left\|H\right\|)`$), the perturbed generator $`F+h`$
remains Legendre (its Hessian stays positive definite on $`K`$).

###### Proof.

Let $`\chi\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,1]`$ be a smooth cutoff (bump)
function with $`\chi(0)=1`$, $`\nabla\chi(0)=0`$, and
$`\supp(\chi)\subset B(0,1)`$ (the unit ball). Let
$`Q_{H}(\pi)=\frac{1}{2}(\pi-q)^{\top}H(\pi-q)`$. Note that
$`\nabla Q_{H}(q)=0`$ and $`\nabla^{2}Q_{H}(\pi)=H`$ for all $`\pi`$.
Given $`\alpha,\delta,U,H`$, we must choose a support-scaling parameter
$`\epsilon>0`$. Define the scaled bump function
$`\chi_{\epsilon}(\pi)=\chi\left(\frac{\pi-q}{\epsilon}\right)`$. We
construct our perturbation $`h`$ by multiplying the desired
$`C^{2}`$-scaling $`\alpha`$ with the product of the bump and the
quadratic form:

|     |                                                  |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       h(\pi)=\alpha\,\chi_{\epsilon}(\pi)\,Q_{H}(\pi).
       ```                                               |     |

We now verify the properties by choosing $`\epsilon`$ appropriately. The
support of $`h`$ is contained in the support of $`\chi_{\epsilon}`$,
which is $`\{\pi\colon\|\pi-q\|<\epsilon\}=B(q,\epsilon)`$, this proves
(1). We can choose $`\epsilon>0`$ small enough such that
$`B(q,\epsilon)\subset U`$. We compute the Hessian using the product
rule.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\nabla^{2}h(\pi)=\alpha\left[(\nabla^{2}\chi_{\epsilon})Q_{H}+(\nabla\chi_{\epsilon})(\nabla Q_{H})^{\top}+(\nabla Q_{H})(\nabla\chi_{\epsilon})^{\top}+\chi_{\epsilon}(\nabla^{2}Q_{H})\right].
``` |  |

At $`\pi=q`$, we have $`\chi_{\epsilon}(q)=\chi(0)=1`$,
$`\nabla\chi_{\epsilon}(q)=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\nabla\chi(0)=0`$, and
$`Q_{H}(q)=0`$. Substituting these values:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\nabla^{2}h(q)=\alpha\left[(\nabla^{2}\chi_{\epsilon}(q))\cdot 0+0+0+1\cdot H\right]=\alpha H.
``` |  |

This proves (3).

The terms in the Hessian are (with $`z=(\pi-q)/\epsilon`$):

- •
  $`\nabla^{2}\chi_{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\nabla^{2}\chi(z)`$.
  This is non-zero only for $`\|\pi-q\|<\epsilon`$.
- •
  $`\nabla\chi_{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\nabla\chi(z)`$.
- •
  $`Q_{H}(\pi)=\frac{1}{2}(\pi-q)^{\top}H(\pi-q)`$. On the support,
  $`\|Q_{H}\|\leq\frac{1}{2}\|H\|\epsilon^{2}`$.
- •
  $`\nabla Q_{H}(\pi)=H(\pi-q)`$. On the support,
  $`\|\nabla Q_{H}\|\leq\|H\|\epsilon`$.

The $`C^{2}`$-norm of $`h`$ is bounded by:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\|\nabla^{2}h\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha\left\|\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}(\nabla^{2}\chi)Q_{H}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}(\nabla\chi)(\nabla Q_{H})^{\top}+\dots+\chi H\right\|_{\infty}
``` |  |

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\leq\alpha\left[\frac{C_{\chi,2}}{\epsilon^{2}}(\tfrac{1}{2}\|H\|\epsilon^{2})+2\frac{C_{\chi,1}}{\epsilon}(\|H\|\epsilon)+C_{\chi,0}\|H\|\right]\leq\alpha\|H\|(C^{\prime}_{\chi,2}+2C^{\prime}_{\chi,1}+C^{\prime}_{\chi,0})
``` |  |

where $`C_{\chi,k}`$ are the $`\sup`$ norms of the $`k`$-th derivatives
of $`\chi`$. Thus,
$`\sup\left\|\nabla^{2}h(\pi)\right\|\leq C\alpha\left\|H\right\|`$ for
$`C=(C^{\prime}_{\chi,2}+\dots)`$, which is a constant depending only on
$`\chi`$. This proves (4).

We compute the gradient:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\nabla h(\pi)=\alpha\left[(\nabla\chi_{\epsilon})Q_{H}+\chi_{\epsilon}(\nabla Q_{H})\right]=\alpha\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon}\nabla\chi(z)\cdot Q_{H}(\pi)+\chi(z)\cdot H(\pi-q)\right]
``` |  |

Using the bounds from (4) for $`z\in B(0,1)`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\|\nabla h(\pi)\|\leq\alpha\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon}C_{\chi,1}(\tfrac{1}{2}\|H\|\epsilon^{2})+C_{\chi,0}(\|H\|\epsilon)\right]=\alpha\left\|H\right\|\cdot\epsilon\cdot(\tfrac{1}{2}C_{\chi,1}+C_{\chi,0})
``` |  |

The gradient norm is
$`\sup\|\nabla h\|\leq C^{\prime}\alpha\left\|H\right\|\epsilon`$. We
are given $`\alpha`$ and $`\delta`$. We can choose $`\epsilon`$ small
enough to satisfy both the support condition
($`B(q,\epsilon)\subset U`$) and the gradient condition:

|     |                                                              |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \epsilon\leq\frac{\delta}{C^{\prime}\alpha\left\|H\right\|}.
       ```                                                           |     |

(If $`\alpha H=0`$, $`h=0`$ and all conditions are trivial). This proves
(2).

Finally, for $`F+h`$ to be Legendre, we need $`\nabla^{2}(F+h)\succ 0`$.
We have $`\nabla^{2}F(\pi)\succeq\lambda I`$. By (4),
$`\|\nabla^{2}h\|_{\infty}\leq C\alpha\left\|H\right\|`$. By choosing
$`\alpha`$ small enough such that $`C\alpha\left\|H\right\|<\lambda`$,
Weyl’s inequality ensures that $`\nabla^{2}F+\nabla^{2}h\succ 0`$ on
$`K`$. ∎

### B.3 Localized approximation of Hessians

###### Lemma 33 (Localized approximation of Hessians).

Let $`F`$ be a Legendre generator with Hessian
$`\nabla^{2}F\colon\Delta\to\mathbb{S}^{d}_{+}`$. For every $`\eta>0`$,
there exists a measurable partition $`\{R_{1},\dots,R_{m}\}`$ of a
subset $`R\subset\Delta`$ with $`\mu(\Delta\setminus R)<\eta`$ such that
for each $`j`$ there exists a symmetric matrix
$`M_{j}\in\mathbb{S}^{d}_{+}`$ satisfying

|     |                                                          |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \sup_{\pi\in R_{j}}\,\|\nabla^{2}F(\pi)-M_{j}\|\leq\eta.
       ```                                                       |     |

In other words, up to a set of measure $`<\eta`$, the Hessian
$`\nabla^{2}F`$ can be approximated within $`\eta`$ by a finite-valued,
piecewise-constant matrix field.

###### Proof.

By Lusin’s theorem, for the given $`\eta>0`$ there exists a compact set
$`K\subset\Delta`$ with $`\mu(\Delta\setminus K)<\eta/2`$ on which
$`\nabla^{2}F`$ is continuous. Since $`K`$ is compact and
$`\nabla^{2}F`$ is continuous on $`K`$, it is uniformly continuous
there. Thus, there exists $`\delta>0`$ such that for all
$`\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in K`$ with
$`\left\|\pi-\pi^{\prime}\right\|\leq\delta`$, we have
$`\|\nabla^{2}F(\pi)-\nabla^{2}F(\pi^{\prime})\|\leq\eta`$.

Cover $`K`$ by finitely many Borel sets $`R_{1},\dots,R_{m}`$ of
diameter at most $`\delta`$. For each $`R_{j}`$, fix a representative
point $`\pi_{j}\in R_{j}`$ and define $`M_{j}=\nabla^{2}F(\pi_{j})`$. By
construction, for all $`\pi\in R_{j}`$ we have
$`\|\nabla^{2}F(\pi)-M_{j}\|\leq\eta`$. Setting
$`R=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}R_{j}`$, we have
$`\mu(\Delta\setminus R)\leq\mu(\Delta\setminus K)<\eta/2<\eta`$.

This yields the desired partition with piecewise-constant approximation.
∎
````
